**house:119:1:145**

Foushee opposed passage of that complete House proposal; no preferred alternative or separate component position is established.

This May22 House passage is the whole H.R.1 reconciliation package, not a separate immigration-only vote. The controlling House choice uses H.Res.436: Rules Committee Print119-3 modified by the amendment printed in H.Rept.119-113 was considered adopted. The retained Immigration clauses are reconstructed against that chain and the contemporaneous as-amended floor Record. The currently served EH incorporates later corrections and renumbering and is a comparison witness for these unchanged selected clauses, not the controlling entire vote-time text. Later engrossment corrections and the July Senate replacement are not silently substituted for this choice. The description selects border infrastructure, detention, State reimbursement and asylum-fee mechanisms; it does not inventory every immigration-related tax, benefit, employment-authorization, child-sponsor or removal provision in this multi-domain package. Section60001 would make additional fiscal2025 CBP appropriations available until September30,2029: $46.5billion for primary/waterborne/secondary barriers, access roads and associated detection attributes; a separate $50million for specified invasive plants impeding RioGrande border operations; and $5billion for CBP facilities/checkpoints near the southwest, northern and maritime borders. These are different purposes, not one immigration-enforcement account or actual expenditures. The adopted manager amendment adds section60004: $12billion in additional fiscal2025 DHS funds, available until September30,2029, for grants reimbursing States for costs of actions on or after January21,2021 assisting federal border/immigration enforcement, including detention/removal and combating unlawful entry of persons/contraband. Applications need satisfactory cost evidence, and DHS cannot reimburse a State already reimbursed under another DHS grant program. This is funding subject to a process and conditions, not proof of State awards or a newly conferred State removal power. Section70101 would appropriate an additional $45billion for fiscal2025 to ICE, available until September30,2029, solely for single-adult alien detention and family residential-center capacity. DHS MAY detain family units pending a removal decision and, if ordered removed, until removal. Its family-center definition includes accompanied alien children, excludes unaccompanied alien children, and expressly applies regardless of State/local facility licensing. Its single-adult detention standards clause says they shall be set in the Secretary's SOLE discretion. Preserve that printed language without importing the Senate's express consistent-with-applicable-law qualification or deciding its interaction with judicial/other legal constraints. The existing unaccompanied-child definition in6USC279(g)(2) requires no lawful immigration status, age under18, and no US parent/legal guardian or none available for care/physical custody; childhood alone does not establish that category. Capacity funding and permission are not a universal detention mandate, removal order or actual detention outcome. Section70002 would require an additional fee at the time an alien files an asylum application: the fiscal2025 amount may be set by rule but cannot be less than $1,000. It cannot be waived/reduced. From fiscal2026 its amount increases using the prior fiscal-year fee and the printed July-to-July CPI-U increase, with the increment rounded down to a multiple of $10. Half of the Attorney-General-filed receipts go to EOIR; half of DHS-filed receipts go to USCIS's existing Immigration Examinations Fee Account; those credited amounts may be retained/spent without further appropriation, and the remainder goes to Treasury. Section70010 separately requires a fee for EACH CALENDAR YEAR an application remains pending, initially at least $100 for fiscal2025, with later CPI-U adjustment, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction. Its printed adjustment has no express rounding term; the filing-fee rounding rule is not copied into it. These are separate filing and pending-application charges, not fixed lifetime amounts or a claim that every asylum applicant actually paid. Section70023 changes existing INA208(d)(3)/8USC1158(d)(3) from MAY impose fees to SHALL, strikes the following cost-cap/installment/nonmandatory-fee language and preserves authority to set additional adjudication/naturalization fees under286(m). The existing paragraph already allowed fees for asylum consideration, employment authorization and asylum-based adjustment and capped them at adjudication costs; the bill does not create first-ever fee authority or establish a new substantive asylum eligibility test through that edit. Existing1356(m)/(n) supply the account/cost-recovery context. Employment-authorization provisions are separate from asylum filing and annual pending-application fees. House70003 would charge an additional fee on initial employment-authorization applications for asylum applicants underINA208(d)(2), paroled aliens and applicants underTPS244(a)(1)(B). Each initial fee has a fiscal2025 floor of$550, may be higher by DHS rule, cannot be waived/reduced and has the printed fiscal2026-onward July-to-July CPI-U increment rounded down to$10. All three initial authorizations would last no more than six months. In70003(a)(4), the House allocation sentence says25percent of fees received UNDER THIS SECTION goes to USCIS's existing fee account to retain/spend without further appropriation, with50percent of that credited amount for benefit-fraud detection/prevention; uncredited amounts go to Treasury. The separately printed parole/TPS subsections direct their fees to Treasury. Preserve those exact scope words rather than silently deciding their interaction. House70012 and70014 separately impose additional parole/TPS employment-authorization renewal or extension fees with fiscal2025$550floors, possible higher rule amounts, subsequent CPI increments rounded down to$10, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction. Both initial and renewed/extended authorizations in those sections last no more than six months. House70013 imposes at least$550 for each asylum-based renewal/extension, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction; that section prints no annual inflation formula. DHS MAY provide asylum-applicant employment authorization for at most six months, and each renewal/extension also has that limit. Authorization terminates immediately after an asylum-officer denial unless referred to an immigration judge;30days after an immigration-judge denial unless timely appealed to BIA; or immediately after BIA denies an appeal of asylum denial. After such termination DHS cannot grant, renew or extend it unless a FederalCourtOfAppeals remands to BIA. This restriction concerns employment authorization, not an automatic asylum denial, immediate removal or a bar on all judicial review. The pre-vote1158(d)(2) already says asylum applicants are not entitled to employment authorization but it may be provided by regulation; applicants not otherwise eligible cannot receive it before180days after asylum filing. Existing1254a(a)(1)(B)/(a)(2) authorizes employment and makes work authorization effective throughout TPS. Fee/document-validity provisions do not themselves end TPS, create first work authority or establish everyone's eligibility. The existing parole paragraph1182(d)(5)(A)/(B) is case-by-case urgent-humanitarian/significant-public-benefit authority subject to the printed statutory exceptions, including the refugee qualification and does not constitute admission. These fees do not supply a grant of parole or automatic employment permission. House70007 imposes a separate additional sponsor-placement fee, collected by HHS from the individual BEFORE placing an unaccompanied alien child underTVPRA235(c). Its fiscal2025 floor is$3,500 or a higher rule amount, with fiscal2026-onward July-to-July CPI-U increments rounded down to$10 and no waiver/reduction. The stated purpose is partial reimbursement for the child's prior federal-custody processing, housing, food, education, transport and other care.25percent goes to HHS to retain/spend without further appropriation for sponsor/adult-household background checks; the remainder goes to Treasury. The specified checks include names, SSNs, dates of birth, validated residence, immigration status and contact information for the sponsor and all adult household residents, and records checks including sex-offender registry, public-records background and fingerprint-based national criminal history. Existing1232(c) already requires protective/suitability assessments, identity/relationship verification, independent risk findings and home studies in specified cases. This is not first-ever sponsor vetting or a new assertion that any sponsor has criminal activity. House70020 imposes ANOTHER additional sponsor fee BEFORE the unaccompanied child's release to the sponsor. Its fiscal2025 floor is$5,000 or a higher rule amount with later CPI increments rounded down to$10, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction. Despite its failure-to-appear title, collection is not conditioned on a child already missing court or having an in-absentia order. At the end of the child's immigration proceedings as an unaccompanied child OR at the end of that sponsorship, HHS MAY reimburse if the sponsor demonstrates the child was not ordered removed in absentia. If such an order was rescinded underINA240(b)(5)(C), the sponsor MAY SEEK reimbursement. Neither reimbursement pathway is an automatic refund. The two sponsor fees remain distinct additional charges; there is no claim that every applicant actually paid them or that payment establishes suitability. Existing1232(c) placement protections and279(g)(2) UAC definition remain material context; their interaction with fee collection is not a finding of actual placement, detention or legal enforceability. House70021 requires a separate fiscal2025 fee of at least$5,000, possibly higher by rule, on an alien BOTH ordered removed in absentia under1229a(b)(5) AND subsequently arrested by ICE. It has later CPI increments rounded down to$10, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction, and does not apply if the underlying order was rescinded under1229a(b)(5)(C). House70022 separately charges any INADMISSIBLE alien at the time CBP apprehends the person BETWEEN PORTS OF ENTRY; its fiscal2025 floor is$5,000 or higher by rule, later CPI increments rounded down to$10, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction. Do not treat every arrest, port-of-entry encounter, unadmitted person or failure to attend court as satisfying these exact fee predicates. The complete pre-vote1229a(b)(5) and(e) retain the written-notice rule and failure-to-provide-address exception, clear/unequivocal/convincing evidence of notice and removability, and separate rescission routes: a motion within180days showing exceptional circumstances beyond the alien's control, or at any time for missing statutory notice or no-fault failure while in federal/state custody. Filing those motions stays removal pending the immigration judge's disposition. Exceptional circumstances include the specified battery/extreme cruelty or serious illness/death examples, excluding less compelling circumstances; removability distinguishes inadmissibility for an unadmitted alien from deportability for an admitted alien.1229(a) preserves notice/address/change-of-hearing context. House70004 adds a parole-entry fee with fiscal2025floor$1,000 or a higher DHS rule amount, fiscal2026-onward July-to-July CPI-U increments rounded down to$10, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction for an applicable fee. It applies to each alien paroled into the US EXCEPT where the alien establishes one of the printed parole-reason exceptions; fee exceptions are not erased by the separate waiver bar. The fee exceptions retain their actual predicates: a medical emergency with necessary treatment unavailable in the foreign state where the alien resides OR a life-threatening emergency with insufficient normal-visa time; the parent/legal guardian of such a medical-emergency MINOR; the alien needed in the US to donate an organ or tissue for transplant, with insufficient normal-visa time; a close family member in the United States whose death is imminent, when normal visa processing would prevent arriving while that person is alive; or seeking to attend a close family member's funeral, when normal visa processing would prevent arriving in the United States in time for it; an adopted child's urgent treatment needed before expected final adoption-related visa issuance while in the petitioner's legal custody; a LAWFUL INA245 adjustment applicant returning after temporary travel abroad; and a person returned to a contiguous country under235(b)(2)(C) paroled to attend the person's immigration hearing. The law-enforcement significant-public-benefit exception requires a DHS determination that significant public benefit has resulted or will result from the parole, BOTH assistance to the USGovernment AND presence required in that matter, PLUS at least one of inadmissibility, failure to meet nonimmigrant-admission eligibility, or insufficient normal-visa time; it is not every asserted public benefit. It is not stated as a fee on every SIJ applicant or an amendment creating SIJ status. House70006 adds a separate TPS-consideration fee with fiscal2025floor$500 or a higher rule amount, later CPI increments rounded down to$10, Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction. Its status predicate is an alien NOTADMITTED to the US OR an admitted NONIMMIGRANT who at TPSapplication time failed to maintain/extend the applicable nonimmigrant status (including authorized-stay compliance) OR comply with its conditions. House 70008 adds a visa-integrity fee on each alien issued a nonimmigrant visa, collected upon issuance in addition to other authorized fees. The fiscal 2025 minimum is $250 or a higher DHS rule amount; fiscal 2026 onward adds the July-to-July CPI-U increment rounded down to the next lowest multiple of $1. Filing is the printed third alternative, not a required grant; nonuse is its own first alternative. House 70009 separately adds a fee on ANY alien applying for Form I-94, with fiscal 2025 minimum $24 or a higher DHS rule amount, later July-to-July CPI-U increments, no expressly printed increment-rounding rule, and no waiver/reduction. House 70017 amends the existing ESTA fee, rather than creating the travel-authorization System or treating it as admission. These are components of the existing sum, not an undifferentiated $10 or $13 total fee. The sunset edit extends only the existing clause(i)(I) Travel Promotion Fund fee restriction from fiscal years beginning after October31,2028 to those beginning after October31,2034. The Great Lakes branch retains its Great Lakes/connecting-waterway and regular-schedule conditions. The repeated scope/cap interaction remains an application question, not silently rewritten as independent per-fee allocations, summed shares, fixed transfers or proven spending. Complete pre-vote1356(m)-(n) establishes the existing account framework without resolving those new competing references. House(l) bars using fees received under the section to fund the Legal Orientation Program OR any successor program; it is a restriction on these receipts, not proof the program ended or all other funding was prohibited. House 10012 replaces SNAP2015(f) with its exact version of individual immigration-status conditions. The replacement is a NECESSARY individual-status condition for a member of an OTHERWISE ELIGIBLE household, not a sufficient promise of SNAP entitlement. US RESIDENCE is common to EACH of the four alternatives, AND the individual must meet at least one listed category: (A) a citizen OR NATIONAL of the UNITED STATES; (B) the defined lawful permanent-resident immigrant, retaining the express exclusion of ordinary temporary visitors/tourists/diplomats/students with no intention of abandoning foreign residence; (C) the version's own status category described below; OR (D) an individual lawfully residing in the US IN ACCORDANCE WITH a Compact of Free Association referred to in PRWORA402(b)(2)(G). Complete1101(a)(15)/(20)/(22) defines immigrant/nonimmigrant classes, unchanged lawful permanent residence and US national, without treating every lawful temporary stay or every foreign national as a listed status. Complete1612(b)(2)(G) identifies the US Compacts with the Federated States of Micronesia, Marshall Islands and Palau; nationality alone is not substituted for the proposal's lawful-residence-under-Compact condition. The complete pre-vote2015(f) already requires residence and lists citizen/LPR, the specified1259 deemed-permanent-residence category, conditional entry under1157/1158, the described1182(d)(5) parole and withholding under1231(b)(3). The amendment replaces that subsection's list: its old conditional-entry/general-parole/withholding categories are no longer separate entries, whereas defined permanent residence remains. The new text expressly says citizen OR USnational and supplies its own(C)/(D); it is not described as the first possible benefit for USnationals or COFA residents. Complete pre-vote1611/1612/1613/1641 separately retain qualified-alien definitions, program-specific restrictions/exceptions, the qualified-status five-year framework with its exceptions, and the2024COFA provisions. Qualification alone was not a complete entitlement, nor does the selected replacement directly amend every one of those separate laws. No blanket claim that every refugee, asylee, parolee or other national loses or gains benefits is made; another independently held listed status, other applicable rules and actual eligibility determination remain distinct. The replacement RETAINS consideration of the INELIGIBLE INDIVIDUAL'S income (less a pro-rata share AT STATE OPTION) AND financial resources when determining the eligibility and allotment value of that individual's household. The State-option modifier concerns the income pro-rata subtraction, not exclusion of all financial resources or automatic termination of all household members. The prior statutory paragraph already has this calculation language; it is not a new proof of spending, a specific household's benefit amount or a benefit award. Whole-package votes do not establish component preferences. House(C) requires Cuban citizenship/nationality AND ALL four own predicates: beneficiary of an APPROVED203(a) petition; meeting ALL immigrant-visa eligibility requirements while a visa is NOT IMMEDIATELY AVAILABLE; NOT OTHERWISE INADMISSIBLE under212(a); AND PHYSICAL PRESENCE IN THE US pursuant to parole furthering the specified US commitment to annual legal Cuban migration in the1994/1995joint migration instruments. Complete pre-vote203(a) family-preference rules and the full proposal clause are bound. This is not every Cuban national, a pending petition, any parole, or an actual grant of a visa. House(C) here does NOT require living in Cuba and does not borrow the distinct House70004 parole-FEE exception's living-in-Cuba predicate. Senate's broader defined Cuban/Haitian-entrant phrase is not imported into this House list. House 44110 changes Medicaid and CHIP verification-period assistance and federal payment beginning October 1, 2026. It adds a State option under 1902(a)(46)(C) to make assistance available during the specified reasonable opportunities for citizenship/nationality or satisfactory immigration-status evidence, and during the separate Social Security inconsistency process's 90-day period. It removes the existing obligation to provide assistance during the specified documentary reasonable-opportunity periods when the State has not elected that option; the 1137(d)(4)(A)(ii)/(B)(ii) no-delay/denial/reduction/termination protection is made conditional on that election for MEDICAID, without applying that new Medicaid qualifier to every other program listed in 1137(b). The opportunity to submit evidence and the program's remaining verification processes are not themselves eliminated. New 1903(i)(22)(B) bars federal payment for amounts expended on assistance furnished under that State election during EACH specified citizenship/nationality reasonable-opportunity period, the 90-day period, or the immigration-status reasonable-opportunity period UNLESS the applicable citizenship/nationality or satisfactory immigration status is VERIFIED BY THE END OF THAT PERIOD. State election alone does not assure federal payment, and verification by the end is not described as sufficient to satisfy every other payment condition. The restriction concerns payment to the State for those expenditures, not a factual determination that missing or inconsistent proof establishes unlawful immigration status or permanent ineligibility. CHIP adds (i)(22) to its incorporated payment restrictions and applies the new State option in the same manner as Medicaid. The complete existing 1902(ee) process distinguishes the first reasonable effort to identify and address clerical/name/number/declaration inconsistencies, with assistance continued WHILE MAKING THAT EFFORT, from the subsequent unresolved-inconsistency process. House section 44110 does NOT strike that first continued-assistance clause. The subsequent 90 days run from the date the INDIVIDUAL RECEIVES the notice, not when the State sends it; that period offers documentary evidence OR resolution with Social Security, with continued assistance conditional on State election. The existing text specifies disenrollment within 30 days AFTER THE END of those 90 days using its printed no-documentary-evidence OR unresolved-inconsistency predicate; the amendment adds denial of eligibility AS APPLICABLE. That unamended printed OR is retained rather than silently replaced with AND or treated as a new resolved legal decision on every combined evidentiary path. Existing appeal rights remain. Other reasonable-opportunity periods are not all converted into a fixed 90-day period. Complete 1903(x) retains its own evidence definitions and exceptions for the specified Medicare-enrolled/entitled, disability-benefit/SSI, foster/adoption, newborn and previously documented categories; this is not a new documentary demand on every citizen. Its (x)(5) preserves the stated first-year deemed-eligibility boundary for a US-born child whose alien mother's delivery is available as emergency treatment. Complete 1137(f) excludes the named emergency-treatment cases from (a)(1) and (d); the selected House clause does not amend 1903(v) emergency-payment predicates. No universal immediate cutoff, eliminated appeal, actual State election, individual award or proven savings is inferred. House section 44111 proposes an immigration-linked change to the EXPANSION matching percentage and its related early-expansion formula, not to every Medicaid matching rate. Its quoted amendment to SSA section 1905(y)(1)(E) adds 80 percent, in place of the existing 90-percent expansion percentage for the specified expenditures, ONLY for calendar quarters beginning on or after October 1, 2027 in which the State is a SPECIFIED STATE with respect to that quarter. Complete pre-vote 1905(y) concerns medical assistance for the defined newly eligible 1902(a)(10)(A)(i)(VIII) group in the 50 States/DC, with its own age, December 1, 2009 eligibility and full-benefit definitions. Complete (z)(2) concerns its distinct early-expansion-State/nonpregnant-childless-adult cohort and transition formula; the proposal changes named year references to quarter references so the formula uses the State's quarter percentage. It is not a ten-point reduction of all State medical spending, a universal flat 80-percent Medicaid rate or an actual identified State's funding outcome. The proposed SPECIFIED STATE definition has two alternative quarter-specific triggers, each applying whether the assistance/coverage is inside or outside the Medicaid plan or waiver/another State program, and REGARDLESS OF FUNDING SOURCE. The financial-assistance alternative requires assistance TO OR ON BEHALF OF an alien who is BOTH not a qualified alien under the proposal's modified definition AND not a lawfully US-residing child or pregnant woman RECEIVING MEDICAL ASSISTANCE UNDER 1903(v)(4), FOR purchasing defined health-insurance coverage FOR an alien who ALSO satisfies BOTH of those exclusions. Both recipient/beneficiary positions retain their own exclusions; lawfully residing child/pregnancy status alone without the printed receipt-of-assistance condition is not substituted for the exemption. The other trigger concerns COMPREHENSIVE HEALTH BENEFITS COVERAGE during the quarter for an alien meeting both exclusions. It does not label every emergency-care expenditure comprehensive coverage or every health expense insurance. Complete PHSA section 2791(a)/(b) defines medical care and health-insurance coverage offered by an issuer under the named policies/service contracts, with its own licensed/regulatory issuer definition. The proposal uses the incorporated alien definition and modifies qualified alien under 1641: it excludes the (b)(4) parole category OTHER THAN the printed qualified-alien-under 402(b)(2) carveout; it shifts the named status-assessment wording from benefit application, receipt or attempted receipt to provision of the specified coverage or financial assistance; and it substitutes the State's opinion in the named battered-individual clauses for the agency-providing-benefits opinion. Complete 1641 and 1612 authorities are bound. This is not an assertion that every parolee is excluded, that every lawful immigrant is a qualified alien, or that any actual State has made the required battered-individual or coverage determination. The governing floor and EH witness retain the literal parenthetical 42 U.S.C. 1395d after SSA section 1905, although the bound Code identifies 1395d as SSA 1812 Medicare benefits and 1396d as SSA 1905 Medicaid definitions/FMAP. The proposed definition's quoted 1905(y)(C) references are shortened relative to the new (y)(2)(C) placement. Neither printed reference is silently repaired or substituted; their application is separately reserved for independent source-grounded review. The complete H.Res. 492 corrections do not correct section 44111. The candidate describes the quoted rate, quarter and defined-State mechanisms with that qualification; it does not claim a resolved legal crosswalk or actual funding loss. The printed wavier typo also remains in the unchanged source, without a new semantic claim. House section 112103 proposes a new Medicare immigration-status restriction with its own Cuban category and existing-enrollee transition. The new section 1899C makes a listed immigration/citizenship category NECESSARY for entitlement to or enrollment in Title XVIII benefits, subject to its own existing-enrollee transition; the list is not sufficient entitlement, an award or proof that a person satisfies all other Medicare requirements. It lists US citizen OR US national, lawful permanent residence under the INA, the version's own category(3), OR lawful US residence in accordance with a referenced Compact of Free Association. Unlike the new SNAP/Medicaid lists, 1899C(a) does not print a NEW COMMON RESIDENCE modifier across every alternative; the COFA alternative keeps its own lawful-residence predicate and other existing Medicare residence rules retain their own scopes. Complete material pre-vote 226/226A/1836 and 1818(a)/1818A(a) are bound. They distinguish hospital entitlement through the named age/insured-benefit, disability/railroad/government-employment or renal-disease pathways from separate Part B or premium Part A enrollment paths. In 1836(a), existing Part A entitlement is an ALTERNATIVE to the age 65/US-resident branch, whose citizen OR LPR alternatives have a five-year continuous-residence condition on the LPR branch. Complete 1818(a) has its own age 65, Part B enrollment, US residence, citizen OR LPR-with-five-years and no-other-Part-A-entitlement conjunction. The five-year modifier is not applied to every citizen, every enrollee or every entitlement path. The limited 1836(b) post-renal immunosuppressive-drug enrollment and own other-coverage exclusions remain distinct from full benefits. Complete 1818A(a) preserves its own disabled/earnings-related premium eligibility, not a generic disability guarantee. Neither age alone nor listed status alone is described as sufficient; the selected proposal does not directly rewrite each underlying benefit rule or establish an exhaustive pre-vote eligibility universe. The transition applies specifically to individuals ENTITLED TO OR ENROLLED FOR Title XVIII benefits AS OF ENACTMENT. The Commissioner must review that cohort to identify people not described in ANY of the four listed alternatives, and notify EACH identified person of termination at the enactment-relative date, as soon as practicable AFTER IDENTIFICATION and in a manner designed to ensure comprehension. The termination date is not measured from receipt or sending of the notice; the review deadline is not the termination date. No identical grace period is promised to every later applicant, perpetual grandfathering, actual completed review, actual termination or observed administrative outcome is inferred. Enactment-relative dates are not converted into dates based on this vote or the later enacted law. House category(3) requires Cuban citizenship/nationality AND ALL four own conditions: an APPROVED 203(a) petition; meeting ALL immigrant-visa eligibility requirements while a visa is NOT IMMEDIATELY AVAILABLE; NOT OTHERWISE INADMISSIBLE under 212(a); AND PHYSICAL US PRESENCE pursuant to parole furthering the specified 1994/1995 annual-legal-migration commitment. It is not every Cuban national, pending petition or any parole, and contains no living-in-Cuba condition borrowed from the separate parole-fee exception. The complete proposed category and incorporated 203(a) baseline are bound. Its transition makes the restriction apply to the existing-enrollee cohort ONE YEAR AFTER ENACTMENT, with the review completed NOT LATER THAN SIX MONTHS AFTER ENACTMENT and the comprehensible notice tied to that one-year termination date. No Senate defined-entrant substitution, 18-month grace or one-year review deadline is imported. House sections 112101/112102 propose their own ACA alien-status and special-income-rule changes. The selected ACA status provision adds lawfully present aliens who are NOT eligible aliens to 36B(e)(1), whose named condition concerns one or more individuals for whom the taxpayer is allowed a section 151 deduction (including taxpayer or spouse), and whose complete existing mechanism reduces the portion of counted premiums attributable to the affected INDIVIDUAL and adjusts family-size/household-income calculations using the specified fraction or comparable method. It is not automatic loss of every otherwise eligible household member's credit or a blanket ban on insurance enrollment. Existing 36B(e)(2) whole-period citizenship/nationality/lawful-presence wording is retained as subparagraph(A); the new eligible-alien provision concerns an individual who IS AN ALIEN AND LAWFULLY PRESENT and who IS, AND IS REASONABLY EXPECTED TO BE FOR THE ENTIRE CREDIT-ENROLLMENT PERIOD, within its own listed LPR, version-specific Cuban-related, OR COFA-lawful-residence category. Both current status and the reasonable whole-period expectation remain, common to the listed alternatives; a single day of status, nationality alone or any lawful temporary stay is not substituted. Citizens/US nationals are not required to fit the new alien-only list. Separate applicable-taxpayer, income, coverage-month, plan/enrollment and other credit requirements remain; status alone is not sufficient. The companion provision deletes 36B(c)(1)(B), whose complete pre-vote special rule requires ALL of household income NOT GREATER THAN 100 percent of the family poverty line, LAWFUL PRESENCE as an alien, AND Medicaid ineligibility BY REASON OF THAT ALIEN STATUS, then treats that taxpayer as applicable with income equal to 100 percent for credit purposes. It is not every Medicaid-ineligible person, any low income or a finding that the person lacks lawful presence. Deletion removes that special treatment for taxable years beginning after December 31, 2025; it does not alone prove that EVERY person at EXACTLY 100 percent loses the credit, because ordinary (c)(1)(A) begins at 100 percent subject to its other rules. The temporary 2021-2025 upper-income rule and complete coverage predicates are separately bound, without extending it into future years or quantifying an actual credit. The status provision's non-conforming amendments apply to TAXABLE YEARS BEGINNING AFTER DECEMBER 31, 2026; its conforming amendments apply to PLAN YEARS BEGINNING ON OR AFTER JANUARY 1, 2027. Those tax/plan-year bases remain distinct from the earlier special-rule repeal; neither vote date nor later enacted implementation dates are substituted. Both status versions replace the lawful-alien phrase in 5000A(d)(3) with the eligible-alien reference; they do NOT change the bound penalty calculation's existing zero-dollar/zero-percent components. No revived monetary mandate penalty or actual tax/benefit determination is inferred. House 36B(e)(2)(B) prints IF AND ONLY IF for eligible-alien treatment. Its Cuban alternative requires Cuban nationality AND ALL approved 203(a)-petition, all-visa-eligibility-but-no-immediate-visa, no-other-inadmissibility and physical-US-presence-under-the-specified 1994/1995-parole-commitment predicates; it has no living-in-Cuba condition from the different parole-fee exception. The common lawful-presence/current-and-whole-period-expectation conditions apply to that alternative too. The Senate's granted entrant definition is not imported. House 112101 conforms 1411 verification for premium credits AND reduced cost-sharing, 1412 federal-payment restriction, 1402(e) individual cost-sharing/family calculations and its own eligible-alien whole-period condition, and 1331(e)(1) basic-health eligible-individual exclusion. The quoted basic-health insertion retains its printed unfinished parenthetical; no governing bytes are repaired. These are direct House amendments to their named programs, not the same scope as the Senate's selected conforming text. Complete underlying program predicates remain: no automatic award for a listed status or whole-household cost-sharing loss is asserted. House 112102 also renumbers the remaining 36B(c)(1) subparagraphs, adjusts its named 2021 cross-reference, removes the lawfully-present/Medicaid-alien-status special branch from basic-health 1331(e)(1)(B), and deletes 1402(b)'s second sentence importing the special credit rule. The cost-sharing and basic-health provisions have their own eligibility/thresholds; their removal is not reduced to a universal tax-credit outcome. House section 112201 proposes its own Exchange verification and filing-compliance conditions. The selected Exchange verification provision adds credit coverage-month conditions for an INDIVIDUAL enrolled in a qualified health plan through an Exchange. A month beginning BEFORE the specified verification is excluded unless the own past-month verification clause is met; this is not an assertion that all earlier months are permanently lost. Applicable information must be PROVIDED OR VERIFIED BY THE APPLICANT, with affirmation of at least the listed information TO THE EXTENT RELEVANT to the specified eligibility. Both versions expressly say failure of this paragraph ALONE does NOT make an individual ineligible to enroll through the Exchange for that month. Credit coverage-month, advance payment, reduced cost-sharing and enrollment are distinct; no insurance-enrollment ban, actual credit award/loss or guaranteed verification date is inferred. Both versions separately exclude a plan from the tax provision's qualified-health-plan definition UNLESS the Exchange PROVIDES a pre-enrollment verification PROCESS through which ANY APPLICANT MAY, BEGINNING NOT LATER THAN AUGUST 1, verify the version-specific information/eligibility for plans in the SUBSEQUENT PLAN YEAR. This is an Exchange process availability deadline, not a requirement that each applicant must submit or complete verification by August 1, a universal enrollment period or a promise of coverage. Amendments in these selected sections apply to TAXABLE YEARS BEGINNING AFTER DECEMBER 31, 2027; the incorporated Senate rule's post-2025 scope is not substituted as the statute's effective date. House coverage-month verification requires ALL eligibility to enroll, for ANY advance premium credit payment, AND for ANY reduced cost-sharing. Its information list separately names income, ANY immigration status, any coverage status/eligibility, residence, family size and authorized other necessary information. Past-month verification prints IF AND ONLY IF and retains all three eligibility branches FOR THAT MONTH. The pre-enrollment process covers enrollment, advance credit AND reduced cost-sharing, rather than the Senate's household-income/enrollment scope. The House filing-compliance clause names the full 45 CFR 155.305(f)(4) AS PUBLISHED MARCH 19, 2025 (90 FR 12942). That publication is a PROPOSED RULE, not the June final rule or a later/current CFR. Its complete printed amendment revises the one-year filing/reconciliation condition and notice alternatives and removes/reserves(ii). The condition requires HHS notification through the specified process that advance payments were made for the tax filer OR either spouse in the married-couple case for the YEAR WHOSE TAX DATA WOULD BE USED for income/family verification, and that the filer or spouse failed to file the required return and reconcile the advances. It is not a filing test for every person regardless of prior advance payments or the named notification/data conditions. The proposed notice alternatives retain tax-information protection: direct notification to the filer of failure and need to file/reconcile immediately on receipt, OR risk/education notification to the filer or enrollee without directly stating the IRS failure indication. No later adopted regulation, determination that the proposal independently became law, automatic household enrollment termination or guaranteed notice-to-loss interval is substituted. The related House section 44201(b)(1) income-verification amendment applies to PLAN YEARS BEGINNING ON OR AFTER JANUARY 1, 2026. If requested Treasury household-income data is NOT RETURNED, income may not be verified SOLELY by that individual's attestation, and the Exchange must take existing 1411(e)(4)(A) actions; it is not an automatic coverage/credit denial. Its specified-income-discrepancy branch requires ALL of an attested projected annual household income qualifying under 36B(c)(1)(A), Treasury OR RELIABLE third-party data indicating income below that applicable-taxpayer floor, an excess over the data by an Exchange-established AND Secretary-approved reasonable threshold of NOT LESS THAN 10 PERCENT (which may ALSO be a dollar amount), AND no Exchange assessment/determination from those data that household income meets the applicable Medicaid OR CHIP income standard. Clause (iii) excludes from THIS DISCREPANCY SUBPARAGRAPH an applicant who is LAWFULLY PRESENT AS AN ALIEN AND Medicaid-ineligible BY REASON OF ALIEN STATUS. It does not exempt all immigrants, every Medicaid-ineligible person or the separate missing-Treasury-data branch. The bound existing 1411(e)/(f) process keeps citizenship/lawful-presence inconsistencies in(e)(3) separate from other-information(e)(4): reasonable clerical/contact effort, then notice and opportunity to provide satisfactory documents OR resolve with the verifying person during 90 days starting WHEN NOTICE IS SENT. The Medicaid individual-receipt clock is not imported. Existing provisional application-based determinations except the citizenship/presence branch, unresolved credit/reduction amount notice and appeals remain distinct from an automatic loss or every issue receiving the same opportunity. The annual filing and automatic-extension provisions are addressed separately below. House section 44201(b)(2)/(3) adds separate annual filing and income-opportunity provisions. Its annual advance-credit rule applies to PLAN YEARS BEGINNING ON OR AFTER JANUARY 1, 2026 for an individual whose prior advance premium credit was paid TO THE PLAN ISSUER for the RELEVANT PRIOR TAX YEAR. That year means the tax year whose return data WOULD BE USED for household-income/family-size verification, not automatically the immediately preceding calendar year. An advance eligibility determination may not be made IF THE EXCHANGE DETERMINES FROM TREASURY INFORMATION that the individual has NOT FILED a return AS REQUIRED under sections 6011/6012 OR, AS NECESSARY, has NOT RECONCILED the advance payment under 36B(f). Those return-required, reconciliation-required, prior-payment and determination conditions are retained; it is not a filing duty for every enrollee or a finding of a particular tax debt. The complete underlying reconciliation mechanism distinguishes credit netting and excess-payment tax treatment with its own pre-vote qualifications, without calculating an actual liability. If an affected individual ATTESTS fulfillment of return filing AND, AS NECESSARY, reconciliation for that relevant year, the Secretary MAY make an INITIAL advance eligibility determination AND MAY DELAY FOR A REASONABLE PERIOD, AS THE SECRETARY DETERMINES, a Treasury-based contrary determination. Attestation does not permanently override Treasury information or guarantee approval. IF the Secretary determines noncompliance AND NOTIFIES THE EXCHANGE, the Exchange must comply with 155.305(f)(4)(i) AS IN EFFECT FOR PLAN YEAR 2025. Its direct protected notice to the filer OR indirect risk/education notice to filer/enrollee is bound at the pre-vote annual text, with the January 2025 addition of (ii) separately checked: that intervening amendment does not change (i). The referenced (i) retains its printed second-consecutive-year risk wording; neither the March proposed immediately-upon-receipt wording nor the June final notice is silently substituted for this DIFFERENT statutory reference. Its relationship to the newly stated annual determination remains qualified rather than a guaranteed delay or an invented notice-to-loss period. The separate(b)(3) provision directs removal of 155.315(f)(7) for PLAN YEARS BEGINNING ON OR AFTER JANUARY 1, 2026, so an Exchange providing the named 90-day inconsistency opportunity may NOT AUTOMATICALLY EXTEND IT ON THE BASIS THAT THE APPLICANT MUST PRESENT SATISFACTORY HOUSEHOLD-INCOME DOCUMENTS. The governed pre-vote (f)(7) supplies that automatic 60-day income-document extension. This is not removal of every opportunity or extension, a universal 90-day cutoff, or a citizenship-status finding: bound (f)(3) separately permits extension if demonstrated GOOD-FAITH EFFORT to obtain documentation, and(f)/(g) retain their own clerical, notice-sent clock, conditional interim-assistance, data/determination and special-circumstance rules. The latter case-by-case documentary exception expressly excludes citizenship/immigration-status inconsistencies. May-vote floor successor-regulation wording remains literal; later House corrections do not replace it. The DACA-specific provisions are addressed separately below; other package components remain unfinished. Complete pre-vote section 1402(g) adds a material boundary to the selected ACA cost-sharing comparison: NO cost-sharing reduction is allowed for coverage for ANY MONTH UNLESS that month is a COVERAGE MONTH FOR WHICH A SECTION 36B CREDIT IS ALLOWED TO THE INSURED OR AN APPLICABLE TAXPAYER ON THE INSURED'S BEHALF. This is a necessary credit/month dependency, not a sufficient entitlement, and it does not require every insured to be the tax filer personally. Shared terms take section 36B meanings; eligibility data use the taxable year of the ADVANCE DETERMINATION, not the taxable year for which the credit is allowed. Retaining a cost-sharing sentence or basic-health branch alone therefore does not establish continuing reductions or an award. Individual/family calculations, own program predicates and the direct House/Senate amendment distinctions remain; residual cross-reference/program application stays RESERVED as question 34. No individual credit, reduction or basic-health outcome is adjudicated. House section 112104 proposes a 3.5 PERCENT remittance tax with its own verification exception, new credit and reporting structure. Both selected remittance sections require the SENDER to pay the tax, the PROVIDER to collect it FROM THE SENDER AND remit QUARTERLY as prescribed by the Secretary, and secondary provider liability ONLY TO THE EXTENT the tax is unpaid at transfer AND not collected. These are separate payer, collection and secondary-liability roles, not a tax only on providers or a charge already observed. Both deem the remittance a financing transaction for the specified multiple-party anti-conduit authority; existing 7701(l) permits appropriate regulation/recharacterization, not an automatic finding of evasion or wrongdoing. Transfer amendments apply to TRANSFERS MADE AFTER DECEMBER 31, 2025, not the vote date or a substituted later enacted date. The bound remittance definitions concern an ELECTRONIC, PROVIDER-INITIATED funds transfer requested by a CONSUMER/NATURAL-PERSON SENDER LOCATED IN A DEFINED STATE to an AUTHORIZED DESIGNATED RECIPIENT LOCATED IN A FOREIGN COUNTRY. Consumer/State, electronic and normal-course provider definitions are independently bound, with account-independence and the statutory SMALL-VALUE EXCLUSION retained. This is not every domestic payment, every cross-border business transaction, every family expense or a recipient-citizenship test. Electronic transfer technology and the Senate physical FUNDING instrument are different predicates. No specific transaction, person, provider, regulatory threshold or actual tax outcome is classified. The House exception requires BOTH a QUALIFIED REMITTANCE PROVIDER with a WRITTEN AGREEMENT WITH THE SECRETARY to verify citizen/national status by prescribed procedures AND a sender VERIFIED BY THAT QUALIFIED PROVIDER AS A US CITIZEN OR NATIONAL pursuant to the agreement. It is not an automatic exception for any asserted citizen, every provider or every SSN holder. Citizen and national remain distinct statutory terms. The House defines remittance terms by printing EFTA SECTION 920(g) alongside 15 USC 1693o-1, while the complete pre-vote Code source credit maps 1693o-1 to SECTION 919 and 1693o-2 card text to SECTION 920. That printed House Act/Code reference mismatch stays literal and separately RESERVED; the Senate's 919(g) is not silently substituted. The available definitions are bound as candidate context, not an adjudicated legal crosswalk. The House separately proposes new 36C: its heading names citizens/nationals, while (a) prints IN THE CASE OF ANY INDIVIDUAL and credits aggregate 4475 taxes PAID BY THAT INDIVIDUAL DURING THE TAXABLE YEAR. No credit is allowed unless the return includes the individual's SSN AND, IF MARRIED, the spouse's SSN, with the defined SSA issuance/due-date restrictions; rules SIMILAR to 32(d) apply. The same House 110004 amendment extends 24(h) and replaces (h)(7), preserving the definition's US-citizen OR named lawful/work-authorized alien issuance branch and BEFORE-DUE-DATE condition. A qualifying number is not proof of US citizenship and not every tax identifier qualifies; child-credit claimant provisions are not imported into 36C. Complete SSA assignment and 32(d) joint-return/separated-spouse baselines are bound without automatically converting SIMILAR rules into identical personal determinations. The credit also requires demonstration TO THE SECRETARY'S SATISFACTION that the 4475 tax WAS PAID BY THE TAXPAYER AND concerned a transfer for which the taxpayer supplied the certification/information referred to in 6050BB(a)(2). Heading versus ANY-INDIVIDUAL operative scope, the marriage/SSN cross-reference and the reporting predicate remain separately reserved for application; no universal citizen refund, automatic noncitizen denial/eligibility or actual credit is asserted. New 6050BB separates aggregate qualified-provider exempt-transfer reporting, individual certified-intent reporting with the printed INFORMATION DESCRIBED IN PARAGRAPH (1) reference and named-person/tax-paid/tax-remitted items, and aggregate other-transfer tax reporting. The provider furnishes a statement to EACH NAMED PERSON as prescribed. The awkward introductory information cross-reference is not repaired into another paragraph, and SSN reporting for every transfer is not inferred. Conforming reporting/refund/math-error text remains literal, without determining penalties or actual refunds. The selected transfer provisions start after December 31, 2025; the new credit and its named conforming amendments instead apply to TAXABLE YEARS ENDING AFTER DECEMBER 31, 2025, not taxable years BEGINNING then. That temporal distinction, explicit substantiation and unresolved printed references prevent an automatic net-cost claim. No Senate cash-only limitation, noncash exemption or lack of credit mechanism is imported into the House version. House section 44201(i) adds three distinct ACA lawful-presence provisions for an ALIEN GRANTED DEFERRED ACTION under the DACA process PURSUANT TO THE NAMED JUNE 15, 2012 MEMORANDUM; satisfying historical criteria, filing a request or having work authorization alone is not the printed grant condition. New 1312(f)(4) says IN THIS TITLE the term alien lawfully present does not include that granted-DACA group. The added 1402(e)(2) sentence says FOR PURPOSES OF THIS SECTION that individual shall not be treated as lawfully present. The added 1412(d) sentence says FOR PURPOSES OF THE PREVIOUS SENTENCE, whose own subject is Federal payments, credits and cost-sharing reductions for individuals not lawfully present. These are separate defined scopes, not revocation of every immigration status, deferral, work permit or insurance policy. Subsection (i)(4) prints that amendments MADE BY THIS SECTION apply WITH RESPECT TO PLAN YEARS BEGINNING ON OR AFTER JANUARY 1, 2026; that wording is preserved alongside the other distinct 44201 dates, without a personal termination date or silently narrowing its section referent. Complete pre-vote 1312(f) concerns qualified individuals and INDIVIDUAL-MARKET QUALIFIED PLANS OFFERED THROUGH AN EXCHANGE, with State residence, custody/pending-disposition distinction and current AND reasonably expected ENTIRE ENROLLMENT PERIOD citizenship/nationality/lawful-presence predicates. Complete 1402(b)/(e) retains its silver-plan, income and individual/family allocation boundaries: a named individual is not a finding that an entire mixed-status household loses coverage or reductions. Complete 1402(g) separately makes an allowed 36B credit for the coverage month, to the insured OR taxpayer on behalf, necessary but not sufficient for reductions. Complete 1412 has its own advance determination/issuer-payment mechanics; its pre-vote State-flexibility sentence permits additional State payments, rather than a guaranteed State award or an adjudicated workaround under the proposed amendments. No universal all-insurance ban, individual benefit loss, effective current regulation or litigation outcome is inferred. The original June 15, 2012 Napolitano memorandum is governed as historical identity and full policy context, from the actual official Supreme Court-hosted signed three-page scan and DOJ Appendix E reproduction. Its five criteria and distinct paths remain source-bound: under-sixteen arrival, continuous residence for the printed five-year period AND presence on the memo date, the listed education/service alternatives, the printed criminal/public-safety criterion and not above thirty. Original wording including "for a least five years" and "or otherwise poses a threat" is retained in the witnesses without inserting a missing word or building a personal eligibility classifier. Background check AND CASE-BY-CASE determination and NO ASSURANCE OF RELIEF remain. ICE/CBP encounter discretion and existing USCIS notice-to-appear guidance differ from ICE pending-removal/no-final-order two-year renewable deferral and the separate USCIS non-removal process with AT LEAST 15 and two-year renewable deferral. Original 60-day implementation directions are relative to the MEMO DATE, not new bill deadlines. The final-order USCIS sentence prints REGARDLESS OF THEIR AGE: its relationship to the minimum age and the other age criterion is RESERVED as question 38, without globalizing the minimum age or declaring all age criteria waived. For an ICE/USCIS deferred-action grantee, USCIS accepts APPLICATIONS TO DETERMINE WHETHER THEY QUALIFY for work authorization during deferral, not an automatic work permit. The memo confers NO SUBSTANTIVE RIGHT, IMMIGRATION STATUS OR PATHWAY TO CITIZENSHIP; that disclaimer does not independently determine ACA lawful-presence treatment. Original policy text is not evidence of a current individual grant, current DACA eligibility/legal validity or current ACA application. Its rationale is not adopted as a claim about any person's motives. Other package provisions and qualifications remain outside these selected highlights. Support/opposition applies only to passage of the entire House proposal; neither component preferences, enactment, legal enforceability nor policy outcomes follow from the vote.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-05-22. This does not establish enactment.

**house:119:1:190**

Foushee opposed concurrence in that complete Senate replacement; no preferred alternative or separate component position is established.

This July3 House concurrence concerns the Senate's July1 EAS, which strikes all after the first word and replaces the earlier H.R.1 text. It is the complete multi-domain reconciliation replacement, not a second passage of the unchanged House bill or a separate vote on its Immigration components. Selected Immigration highlights here concern border infrastructure, detention and asylum fees; other immigration-related benefits, taxes, employment authorization, child-sponsor, reimbursement and enforcement provisions are not exhaustively described. Section90001 would appropriate an additional $46.55billion for fiscal2025 to CBP, available until September30,2029, for new/replacement primary, waterborne and secondary barriers, access roads, detection attributes and ground preparation for CBP operations and barrier construction/maintenance. The different House60001 accounts are not silently used to define this replacement amount or its purposes. Section90003 would appropriate an additional $45billion for fiscal2025 to ICE, available until September30,2029, for single-adult alien detention and family residential-center capacity. Aliens MAY be detained at family centers pending an INA removal decision and, if ordered removed, until removal. Single-adult detention standards are set in the Secretary's discretion CONSISTENT WITH APPLICABLE LAW. The family-center definition covers apprehended/encountered family units, including children who are not statutorily defined unaccompanied alien children, and does not reproduce the House's express regardless-of-State-licensing clause.6USC279(g)(2) requires no lawful immigration status, under18 age, and no US parent/legal guardian or none available for care/physical custody. Neither the capacity funding nor permission establishes automatic detention/removal of every alien, elimination of all legal constraints or actual operations. Section100002 requires an additional fee upon filing an asylum application. The fiscal2025 amount is the greater of $100 or the amount set by DHS/the Attorney General, as applicable, by rule; it cannot be waived/reduced. From fiscal2026 the prior fiscal-year fee is increased by the printed July-to-July CPI-U increment rounded down to a multiple of $10. Half of Attorney-General-filed receipts go to EOIR; half of DHS-filed receipts go to USCIS's existing Immigration Examinations Fee Account, retained/expended without further appropriation; uncredited receipts go to Treasury. This $100 filing floor differs from the House's $1,000 floor. Section100009 separately requires an additional fee for EACH CALENDAR YEAR an asylum application remains pending. Its fiscal2025 amount is the greater of $100 or a DHS rule amount, followed by the printed CPI-U annual adjustment with the increment rounded DOWN TO THE NEAREST DOLLAR; receipts go to Treasury and the fee cannot be waived/reduced. Do not transfer that rounding to the initial-filing fee or back to the House's differently printed yearly formula. Section100018 changes pre-vote INA208(d)(3)/8USC1158(d)(3) from MAY impose fees to SHALL and replaces the following cost-cap/installment/nonmandatory-fee language with preserved additional adjudication/naturalization-fee authority under286(m). The baseline already authorized asylum-consideration, employment-authorization and asylum-based adjustment fees;1356(m)/(n) govern existing account/cost-recovery context. No first-ever fee authority, new asylum eligibility test from that edit, specific implemented collection rule or actual payment is inferred. Employment-authorization fees are separate from asylum filing and annual pending-application fees. Senate100003 requires additional initial application fees for asylum applicants under1158(d)(2), paroled aliens andTPS employment-authorization applicants under1254a(a)(1)(B). Each fiscal2025 floor is$550 or a higher DHS rule amount, with no waiver/reduction and fiscal2026-onward July-to-July CPI-U increments rounded down to$10. The asylum-initial subsection contains no House-style six-month duration sentence; it credits25percent of fees collected PURSUANT TO THIS SUBSECTION to USCIS's existing fee account, retained/expended without further appropriation with NOT LESS THAN50percent of the credited share for benefit-fraud detection/prevention, and sends the rest to Treasury. Parole/TPS initial fees go to Treasury; their initial authorizations last one year or the duration of parole/TPS respectively, whichever is shorter. Senate100010 and100012 set separate parole/TPS employment-authorization renewal/extension fiscal2025 fee floors of$275, possible higher DHS rule amounts and later CPI increments rounded down to$10. Each sends25percent to USCIS's existing fee account to retain/spend without further appropriation and the rest to Treasury; no waiver/reduction.100012 states one year or the duration of the designation ofTPS, whichever is shorter.100010 literally says employment authorization for each paroled alien, OR ANY RENEWAL OR EXTENSION OF SUCH PAROLE, shall be valid for one year or parole duration, whichever is shorter. Preserve this printed antecedent and leave its application unresolved; do not rewrite it into a new parole-eligibility rule or import House six-month text. Senate100011 imposes at least$275 for each asylum-based employment-authorization renewal/extension, with25percent credited to USCIS's existing fee account without further appropriation, the rest to Treasury and no waiver/reduction. It prints no annual CPI formula or House-style six-month limit. It retains the three termination points: immediately after asylum-officer denial unless referred to an immigration judge;30days after immigration-judge denial unless timely appealed to BIA; immediately after BIA denies an appeal of asylum denial. It does not reproduce the separate House70013(d) prohibition on later grants/renewals/extensions absent a FederalCourtOfAppeals remand. Absence of that House sentence does not establish unlimited permission or erase other law. The complete pre-vote1158(d)(2) already permits regulated asylum-based work authorization while denying entitlement and preserving a180day wait for applicants not otherwise eligible. Existing1254a(a)(1)(B)/(a)(2) authorizes employment throughout TPS. Document-validity clauses do not themselves terminate TPS, create first employment authority or establish individual eligibility. Existing1182(d)(5)(A)/(B) preserves case-by-case temporary humanitarian/significant-public-benefit parole subject to the printed statutory exceptions, including its refugee qualification and the distinction from admission; paying a fee does not supply parole or work permission. Senate100016 requires a fiscal2025 fee of at least$5,000, or a higher DHS rule amount, on an alien BOTH ordered removed in absentia under1229a(b)(5) AND subsequently arrested by ICE, with fiscal2026-onward CPI-U increments rounded down to$10. It does not apply if the order was rescinded under1229a(b)(5)(C).50percent goes to ICE's Detention and Removal Office Fee Account, retained/expended without further appropriation; the rest goes to Treasury. It expressly bars waiver/reduction. These fee conditions preserve the underlying notice/address/removability proof, rescission, exceptional-circumstances definition and motion-to-reopen stay in complete pre-vote1229a(b)(5)/(e) and1229(a); an actual order/arrest, new removal ground or denied legal review is not inferred. Senate100017 separately requires an additional fee from any INADMISSIBLE alien at the time the person is apprehended BETWEEN PORTS OF ENTRY. Its fiscal2025 floor is$5,000 or a higher DHS rule amount, with later CPI-U increments rounded down to$10. Unlike House70022, this operative sentence does not name CBP as the apprehending actor. It prints TWO proceeds clauses: subsection(c) sends50percent to ICE's Detention and Removal Office Fee Account for retention/spending without further appropriation and the remainder to Treasury; subsection(d) says ALL fees under the section go to Treasury. The exact EAS HTML and officialPDF pages827-828 both print these clauses. The candidate preserves the apparent allocation conflict and does not choose an operative distribution. No express no-waiver clause is printed in100017; do not import100016(e) or House70022(d), or infer that a waiver is available. These are exact between-port/inadmissibility and in-absentia-plus-ICE-arrest fee predicates, not all enforcement encounters or a fee-imposed finding that an alien actually met any predicate. Senate100004 adds a parole-entry fee with fiscal2025floor$1,000 or higher DHS rule amount, subsequent CPI-U increments rounded down to$10 and Treasury disposition. Its exceptions require the alien to establish the parole reason to DHS's satisfaction on an INDIVIDUAL,CASE-BY-CASE basis; its no-waiver clause expressly preserves those exceptions. The fee exceptions retain their actual predicates: a medical emergency with necessary treatment unavailable in the foreign state where the alien resides OR a life-threatening emergency with insufficient normal-visa time; the parent/legal guardian of such a medical-emergency MINOR; the alien needed in the US to donate an organ or tissue for transplant, with insufficient normal-visa time; a close family member in the United States whose death is imminent, when normal visa processing would prevent arriving while that person is alive; or seeking to attend a close family member's funeral, when normal visa processing would prevent arriving in the United States in time for it; an adopted child's urgent treatment needed before expected final adoption-related visa issuance while in the petitioner's legal custody; a LAWFUL INA245 adjustment applicant returning after temporary travel abroad; and a person returned to a contiguous country under235(b)(2)(C) paroled to attend the person's immigration hearing. The law-enforcement significant-public-benefit exception requires a DHS determination that significant public benefit has resulted or will result from the parole, BOTH assistance to the USGovernment AND presence required in that matter, PLUS at least one of inadmissibility, failure to meet nonimmigrant-admission eligibility, or insufficient normal-visa time; it is not every asserted public benefit. Senate exception9 instead requires that the alien HASBEENGRANTED the status of Cuban/Haitianentrant as defined inPL96-422Sec501(e). That complete definition distinguishes special Cuban/Haitianentrant-parole/subsequently-established special-status categories from OTHERCuban/Haitian nationals with the listed parole/no-other-status, removal-proceeding or pending-asylum alternatives AND no final,nonappealable,legally-enforceable removal order. Its status-at-assistance/services wording is preserved in the bound definition, not silently turned into proof of the fee-specific grant condition or a nationality-only exemption. Do not use the different House Cuba-residence/family-petition/program conjunction to define this replacement, or infer actual parole/fee exemption from nationality. Existing1182(d)(5) authority/statutory exceptions/non-admission distinction remain; paying or excepting a fee does not grant parole, admission or adjustment. Senate100005 requires an additional SIJapplication fee from ANYALIEN,PARENT,ORLEGALGUARDIAN of an alien applying under1101(a)(27)(J), with fiscal2025floor$250 or a higher DHS rule amount, later CPI increments rounded down to$10 and Treasury disposition. It does not print the House one-viable-parent fee predicate or the House express waiver bar; no waiver availability or separate cumulative charges on every named payer are inferred. Complete1101(a)(27)(J) retains the juvenile-court dependency/custody and1ORboth-parent nonviability findings, best-interest-against-return finding, DHSconsent, HHS-specific custody-jurisdiction consent and no derivative parentage rights. These fee provisions do not grant SIJstatus, import a UAC-under18 definition or alter those eligibility findings by themselves. Senate100006 edits EXISTING1254a(c)(1)(B) registration authority: it raises the statutory fee MAXIMUM from$50 to$500, subject to fiscal2026-onward CPI-U adjustments rounded down to$10. It leaves the existing AttorneyGeneral MAYrequire a REASONABLE registration fee language and separate work-document fee authority; this is not a mandatory universal$500charge, a$500minimum, the House conditional additional fee or the separate$550initial/$275renewal EADfees. The amendment adds Treasury disposition and no waiver/reduction of required fees. The existing appropriation-credit sentence is NOTEXPRESSLYSTRUCK in the printed edit and remains in redesignatedclause(i), alongside addedclause(iii) directing all fees under the subparagraph to Treasury. Preserve that apparent proceeds interaction for legal/application review rather than silently deleting the baseline sentence or choosing an implemented distribution. The proposed registration-cap change does not grant or end TPS, guarantee status eligibility, prove payment or establish an individual's outcome. Senate 100007 adds a visa-integrity fee payable by any alien issued a nonimmigrant visa at issuance, in addition to other authorized fees. The fiscal 2025 minimum is $250 or a higher DHS rule amount; subsequent July-to-July CPI-U increments are rounded down to the nearest dollar. Unreimbursed fees go to Treasury and the applicable fee cannot be waived or reduced. DHS MAY reimburse after the visa period of validity expires only if the alien demonstrates compliance with ALL visa conditions after admission, including no unauthorized employment, AND one of two printed alternatives: (A) did not seek to extend the period of admission during the visa validity period AND departed no later than five days after the last day of "such period"; OR (B) during that validity period WAS GRANTED an extension of nonimmigrant status or adjustment to lawful permanent resident status. The text uses "such period" in alternative (A); its application is reserved rather than silently choosing visa validity or authorized admission as the referent. The House nonuse, filing-only and change-of-status alternatives are not imported into this version. A filing is not a grant; reimbursement remains discretionary and conditional, not guaranteed repayment or a general permission to stay/work. The complete pre-vote 1101(a)(26) definition distinguishes proper nonimmigrant visa issuance from actual admission, compliance or an individual outcome. Senate 100008 separately adds an I-94 application fee on any alien submitting that application, with fiscal 2025 minimum $24 or higher DHS rule amount, later July-to-July CPI-U increments rounded down to the nearest dollar and no waiver/reduction. Twenty percent of collected fees goes to the existing Land Border Inspection Fee Account pursuant to 1356(q)(2), available to CBP to retain and spend without further appropriation for I-94 processing; amounts not deposited there go to Treasury. The complete pre-vote subsection (q) establishes the account and its land-port receipt/expense/report framework. That baseline account scope is not substituted for the new section's any-alien I-94 application predicate, and its appropriations refunds are not applicant reimbursements. The visa fee and I-94 fee have separate collection events and dispositions; neither proves actual admission, visa compliance, a collection or immigration-status outcome. Senate 100014 amends the existing ESTA fee components under complete pre-vote1187(h)(3): the existing $17 per travel authorization remains, the separate full-System-cost-recovery component gains a $10 MINIMUM while retaining that cost condition, and a new component is at least $13 PER TRAVEL AUTHORIZATION. This does not describe a $10 or $13 total or first creation of ESTA. Its sunset edit extends the clause(i)(I) Travel Promotion Fund fee restriction from fiscal years beginning after October31,2028 to those beginning after October31,2034; it is not a sunset of every component. Its fiscal2026-onward CPI-U adjustment names the clause(i)(II) amount but literally uses the prior fee required UNDER THIS SUBPARAGRAPH as its base. That component/base relationship remains a separate application question, not a silently substituted isolated cost component or definite whole-fee adjustment; no increment-rounding rule is printed. Unlike selected House70017, this section does not edit existing clause(ii) disposition language: that baseline directs (i)(I) to the Travel Promotion Fund and (i)(II) to Treasury for System administration, but does not assign newly added (i)(III) by name. The new $13 component's disposition is separately reserved, without copying the House Treasury sentence or claiming to have closed other package funding provisions. Exact officialPDFpages819-821 visually confirm the printed adjustment language and section boundary; no HTML-parser repair is made. Existing travel-eligibility/admissibility/visa-eligibility distinctions remain, and no express new ESTA fee-waiver prohibition or inferred waiver availability is imported from EVUS. Senate 100015 separately adds an EVUS fee at enrollment for any alien SUBJECT to that System, additional to other authorized fees, with fiscal2025 minimum $30 or a higher DHS rule amount, later CPI-U increments rounded down to $0.25 and no waiver/reduction. It inserts a new1356(w) CBP EVUS Account with deposits equal to fees received MINUS $5 multiplied by the NUMBER OF PAYMENTS collected; that same $5-times-payments amount goes to Treasury. This payment-count language is retained rather than substituted with House70019's per-enrollment wording or inferred one-payment-per-enrollment implementation. Account amounts are appropriated without further appropriation and available until expended for CBP EVUS administration costs. OfficialPDF823 confirms the payment-count basis. The fee does not expand who is subject to EVUS, establish admission or visa/status eligibility, prove compliance or document an actual payment. Senate 100013 adds ten EOIR fee provisions despite its narrower adjustment-of-status section title. In(a), an alien who files the specified adjustment application with immigration court OR whose application is adjudicated there pays at filing OR before court adjudication. In(b)/(c), the fee is required from any alien at court filing of the inadmissibility-waiver/TPS application OR before court adjudication. No automatic duplicate charge at both events is asserted. The(d)/(e) alien appeals, (f) practitioner appeal and(g) alien motion collect at filing. The(h) suspension application and(i) application for an alien who is a lawful permanent resident collect at court filing. Senate(j) separately requires a payer who IS NOT A LAWFUL PERMANENT RESIDENT at the time that payer files with immigration court a cancellation/adjustment application FOR ANY ALIEN; this wording is not silently collapsed into House's application-category wording or an assumed identity of payer and beneficiary. The ten listed fiscal2025 floors are separate fee events, additional to other authorized fees, with a higher AttorneyGeneral rule amount permitted: adjustment to lawful permanent residence $1,500; waiver of grounds of inadmissibility $1,050; TPS $500; appeal from an immigration-judge decision $900; appeal from a DHS-officer decision $900; practitioner disciplinary appeal $1,325; motion to reopen or reconsider an immigration-judge or Board of Immigration Appeals decision $900; suspension of deportation application $600; cancellation of removal for the specified permanent-resident category $600; and cancellation/adjustment for the specified nonpermanent-resident category $1,500. Each subsection has fiscal2026-onward July-to-July CPI-U adjustment of its own prior-year fee, with the INCREMENT rounded down to $10, not an automatically exact future price or a sum charged to every alien. The disciplinary appeal payer is a PRACTITIONER filing an appeal of an adjudicating official's decision in a practitioner disciplinary case, not every alien or every attorney. The immigration-judge appeal provision separately excludes appeals of BOND decisions. The motion-fee exception identifies only motions to REOPEN an IN-ABSENTIA removal order under240(b)(5)(C)(ii) or an in-absentia deportation order under legacy242B(c)(3)(B), with the version's own printed temporal wording retained below. Complete pre-vote1229a(b)(5)(C)(ii) allows the identified motion at any time upon demonstrating either lack of notice in accordance with1229(a)(1) OR(a)(2), OR Federal/State custody AND nonappearance through no fault of the alien. Complete historical1252b(c)(3)(B) likewise allows the identified motion at any time upon demonstrating lack of notice in accordance with its(a)(2), OR Federal/State custody AND nonappearance through no fault of the alien. The surrounding notice/address requirements remain; these are not exemptions for every missed hearing, any custody, all reopening/reconsideration motions, or the separate180-day exceptional-circumstances grounds in240(b)(5)(C)(i)/242B(c)(3)(A). A fee exception does not establish that a motion was granted or an order actually rescinded. The1995Code historical section is corroborated by the later repeal/history notice and enacted104-208: its intervening371(b)(7) changes only242B(d)(1) officer terminology, not(c)(3)(B), and its309 effective-date/transition provisions remain qualified. Current repeal metadata is not substituted for operative legacy text. Senate(g)(3)(B) literally ends with a motion filed in accordance with242B(c)(3)(B) PRIOR TO APRIL1,1997, without House's explicit AS THE SECTION EXISTED phrase. The printed date attachment remains an application question: no definite pre1997 filing cutoff or deletion of the date is accepted, and the underlying legacy statute's at-any-time text remains visible. OfficialPDF812 visually confirms the same wording; it is not repaired as a parser error. Senate100013 prints NO express section-wide no-waiver/reduction clause; House(k) is not imported and availability of a waiver is not inferred. Every(a)-(j) disposition instead names fees collected pursuant to THIS SUBSECTION, with a not-more-than25percent ExaminationsFeeAccount transfer/EOIR retention-and-spending cap and amounts not transferred/credited toTreasury; Senate(a) does not copy House's50percent cap. These ceilings are not mandatory actual percentages, direct refunds to applicants or new account creation. Senate(k) bars EOIR expenditure of section-collected fees for the Legal Orientation Program OR any successor, without establishing termination or a universal funding ban. No component preference follows from either member's whole-package vote. Senate 10108 replaces SNAP2015(f) with its own exact individual-status list. The replacement is a NECESSARY individual-status condition for a member of an OTHERWISE ELIGIBLE household, not a sufficient promise of SNAP entitlement. US RESIDENCE is common to EACH of the four alternatives, AND the individual must meet at least one listed category: (A) a citizen OR NATIONAL of the UNITED STATES; (B) the defined lawful permanent-resident immigrant, retaining the express exclusion of ordinary temporary visitors/tourists/diplomats/students with no intention of abandoning foreign residence; (C) the version's own status category described below; OR (D) an individual lawfully residing in the US IN ACCORDANCE WITH a Compact of Free Association referred to in PRWORA402(b)(2)(G). Complete1101(a)(15)/(20)/(22) defines immigrant/nonimmigrant classes, unchanged lawful permanent residence and US national, without treating every lawful temporary stay or every foreign national as a listed status. Complete1612(b)(2)(G) identifies the US Compacts with the Federated States of Micronesia, Marshall Islands and Palau; nationality alone is not substituted for the proposal's lawful-residence-under-Compact condition. The complete pre-vote2015(f) already requires residence and lists citizen/LPR, the specified1259 deemed-permanent-residence category, conditional entry under1157/1158, the described1182(d)(5) parole and withholding under1231(b)(3). The amendment replaces that subsection's list: its old conditional-entry/general-parole/withholding categories are no longer separate entries, whereas defined permanent residence remains. The new text expressly says citizen OR USnational and supplies its own(C)/(D); it is not described as the first possible benefit for USnationals or COFA residents. Complete pre-vote1611/1612/1613/1641 separately retain qualified-alien definitions, program-specific restrictions/exceptions, the qualified-status five-year framework with its exceptions, and the2024COFA provisions. Qualification alone was not a complete entitlement, nor does the selected replacement directly amend every one of those separate laws. No blanket claim that every refugee, asylee, parolee or other national loses or gains benefits is made; another independently held listed status, other applicable rules and actual eligibility determination remain distinct. The replacement RETAINS consideration of the INELIGIBLE INDIVIDUAL'S income (less a pro-rata share AT STATE OPTION) AND financial resources when determining the eligibility and allotment value of that individual's household. The State-option modifier concerns the income pro-rata subtraction, not exclusion of all financial resources or automatic termination of all household members.
