### H.R.30 sex-offense and domestic-violence immigration grounds

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.30 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.30 would add specified sex/domestic-violence-related inadmissibility grounds, add conviction-based sex-offense deportability within the admitted-person framework, and broaden the existing domestic-violence definition.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:17**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.30 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact House-passed H.R.30 adds INA 212(a)(2)(J) inadmissibility for a person convicted of, admitting commission of, or admitting acts constituting the essential elements of a sex offense under 34 USC 20911(5), or a conspiracy to commit one. New 212(a)(2)(K) uses those same conviction/admission predicates for a crime of domestic violence (defined through 237(a)(2)(E)), stalking, child abuse/neglect/abandonment, or a crime violating the protective-order portion addressing credible threats, repeated harassment or bodily injury. The INA baseline makes these inadmissibility grounds relevant to visas and admission; the bill is not limited by its title to persons unlawfully present. It separately adds INA 237(a)(2)(G) deportability for conviction of a defined sex offense or conspiracy: admission of conduct alone is not the predicate in this new deportability clause. The existing 237(a)(2)(E)(i) after-admission conviction ground for domestic violence, stalking and child abuse/neglect/abandonment remains; its domestic-violence definition is expanded to include any crime constituting domestic violence under 34 USC 12291(a), regardless of whether the jurisdiction receives grants. Noncriminal behavior mentioned in the victim-services portion of that definition is not itself converted into a crime by this bill. The incorporated sex-offense definition covers criminal sexual-act/contact offenses, specified offenses against minors, listed federal/military offenses, and attempts/conspiracies, with its foreign-conviction fairness and consensual-conduct limitations. The latter exclude specified consensual adult conduct unless custodial authority exists, and specified conduct where the victim is at least 13 and the offender is not more than 4 years older. Some covered conduct already can trigger existing criminal inadmissibility or deportability grounds; these are additional grounds and a definition expansion, not the creation of all immigration consequences for these crimes. INA 101(a)(48) supplies the existing conviction framework, including specified withheld adjudications with a guilt/plea/facts predicate plus court-ordered punishment, penalty or restraint. H.R.30 references 20911(5), rather than expressly importing the separate 20911(8) juvenile-conviction definition. The bill leaves the 237(a)(7) discretionary battered-person waiver for specified domestic-violence/stalking/protective-order deportability grounds intact; that waiver is not written to cover the new sex-offense ground. Existing 212(d)(3) discretionary temporary nonimmigrant admission and 212(d)(13)/(14) T/U status waiver authorities remain within their own conditions. Existing 212(h) enumerates other criminal grounds and is not expanded to the newJ/K grounds. No universal waiver or automatic removal is established. The new 237(a)(2) deportability ground sits within the existing 237(a) framework for an alien (including a crewman) in and admitted to the United States, with removal upon the specified government order. It is not a universal new deportability ground for every unadmitted alien. INA 101 distinguishes immigration admission (lawful entry after inspection/authorization) from admitting criminal conduct; parole alone is not admission. This governing status scope is separate from any offense's after-admission timing element. No individual admission status or actual removal order is inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-01-16. This does not establish enactment.

- INA 237(a) deportability concerns an alien in and admitted to the United States, subject to the governing order framework. Immigration admission and admissions of criminal conduct are different conditions; parole alone is not immigration admission. The new numbered ground does not discard this governing chapeau or necessarily add an after-admission-crime timing element.
- The new inadmissibility clauses permit conviction or specified admissions. The new sex-offense deportability clause requires conviction. The existing domestic-violence/stalking/child-crime deportability clause retains its after-admission conviction condition; the existing protective-order clause separately depends on a court determination.
- Sex-offense scope preserves 20911(5) foreign-conviction safeguards and consensual-conduct exclusions, including the at-least 13/not-more-than 4 years-older condition. The separate 20911(8) juvenile definition is not automatically treated as incorporated by the reference to(5); no individual juvenile-case conclusion is made.
- The additional domestic-violence definition still requires a crime. Victim-service definitions that also describe noncriminal coercive conduct do not alone satisfy that predicate. Grant receipt is expressly irrelevant to the added definition.
- The existing 237(a)(7) waiver requires a battered/extreme-cruelty victim who was not the primary perpetrator, and specified self-defense/protective-order/connected-crime conditions; the connected-crime route excludes serious bodily injury. The Attorney General retains credible-evidence discretion. This limited waiver does not extend automatically to new 237(a)(2)(G).
- Existing inadmissibility exceptions and waivers retain their own enumerated scope. The 212(a)(2)(A) single-offense minor/petty-offense exceptions and 212(h) waiver are not stated as exceptions to newJ/K; retained 212(d)(3),T andU routes are discretionary and conditioned, rather than categorical relief.
- This whole-bill House passage choice does not establish separate component preferences, wrongdoing, actual visa refusal, admission denial, deportation or enactment. The bill text addresses any alien meeting the grounds, rather than only unlawful presence suggested by its short title.

### H.R.2056 D.C. immigration-information and lawful-request restrictions

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.2056 with those prohibitions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.2056 would prohibit D.C. policies restricting specified citizenship/immigration-status information exchange or compliance with lawful DHS detainer/release-notice requests under INA 236 or 287.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:171**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.2056 with those prohibitions; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact H.R.2056 EH proposal prohibits the District of Columbia from having a statute, ordinance, policy or practice that prohibits or restricts a District government entity or official from sending, receiving, maintaining or exchanging information about any individual's citizenship or immigration status, lawful or unlawful, with any Federal, State or local government entity. It separately prohibits such District restrictions on compliance with a lawfully made DHS request under INA 236 or 287 to comply with a detainer for, or notify about release of, an individual. This is an express D.C.-specific restriction on governmental policies/practices. Existing 8 USC 1373 and 1644 already contain statutory restrictions on limiting certain status-information exchanges with the immigration service; the bill is not the first general permission to communicate immigration status. The bill's printed information clause includes citizenship as well as status and communication with any listed governmental entity, while its separate lawful-request clause specifically addresses detainers/release notices. INA 236 and 287 already govern qualified immigration custody and officer powers, including specified custody/release conditions, arrest limits and cooperation provisions. H.R.2056 does not itself rewrite those authorities, deem every DHS request lawful, or establish that every individual detainer must be obeyed. It bars District policies restricting the specified lawful-request compliance; individual custody legality, actual information disclosure/detention/removal, implementation and enactment are not inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-06-12. This does not establish enactment.

- The regulated actor is the District of Columbia and its restrictive statutes, ordinances, policies or practices. The covered information is citizenship/immigration status (lawful or unlawful), not all personal information. No nationwide mandate on every State/local jurisdiction is stated.
- The request must be lawfully made by DHS under INA 236 or 287 and concern detainer compliance or release notification. The bill does not independently supply unlimited arrest/detention power or declare a particular request lawful.
- Existing status-information provisions in 8 USC 1373/1644 and qualified INA officer/custody authorities predate the proposal. The new D.C.-specific policy prohibition is distinguished from those statutory baselines; the candidate does not adjudicate their constitutional enforceability.
- This whole-bill passage choice does not establish a component preference, actual compliance, disclosure, detention/removal, a court outcome or enactment.

### H.R.1998 conditional piracy-related immigration sanctions

**Compact:** Foushee supported passage of H.R.1998 as a whole with its source-qualified provisions. H.R.1998 would impose qualified piracy-related visa/entry/INA-benefit restrictions and entry-document revocation; property blocking is discretionary, and the existing piracy offenses, exceptions and national-security waiver limit the proposal.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:172**

Foushee supported passage of H.R.1998 as a whole with its source-qualified provisions.

The complete H.R.1998 EH creates a conditional sanctions scheme separate from its findings and nonbinding global anti-piracy statements. The President must impose the described sanctions for a foreign person determined knowingly to engage in piracy. Foreign person excludes the printed U.S.-person categories: U.S. citizens, permanent resident aliens and U.S.-organized entities including foreign branches; unlike H.R.36 this definition does not exclude every person merely located in the United States. Section4(b)(1) property blocking is MAY despite the general SHALL in4(a), and dispenses with1701 emergency prerequisites. The immigration route applies to an INA alien whom State or DHS, or either designee, knows or has reason to believe is described in4(a): inadmissibility, no visa/entry documentation, no admission/parole or any other INA benefit. The issuing consular officer, State, DHS or a designee SHALL revoke the qualifying alien visa/entry documentation in accordance with existing INA221(i), regardless of issuance date, with immediate effect cancelling other valid entry documents. Existing221(i) already gives consular officers/State discretionary anytime revocation, notice to the Attorney General, invalidation from issuance, protected carrier reliance absent due notice, and the qualified judicial-review limitation/sole-ground removal-proceeding exception. The new targeted mandatory rule and listed DHS actor are not the first visa-revocation authority; the candidate does not infer repeal of the incorporated safeguards. Piracy expressly means ANY act violating ANY provision of18 USCchapter81, not only a violent seizure at sea. The chapter includes law-of-nations/high-seas piracy, citizenship/treaty-qualified foreign commissions or war, privateer arming/service, violent obstruction of a commander, vessel/property conversion or surrender, corrupt assistance/trading/confederacy, distress-vessel plunder, escape obstruction/false lights, malicious vessel attack, knowing receipt of pirate property and robbery ashore from a piratical cruise, each with its own elements/jurisdiction. These are existing predicates, not new criminal offenses or a claim that every offense can apply to every foreign person; the bill determination/knowledge test does not expressly require conviction. Necessary admission OR parole for UN-headquarters/other international obligations is excepted, with the printed reference to sanctions with respect to admission retained. Qualified agricultural/food/medicine/device, humanitarian, finance and necessary transport transactions are excepted, as are authorized U.S. intelligence, law-enforcement and national-security activities. No importation-of-goods sanctions authority is added and no family extension is stated. The President may waive sanctions after certifying to House Foreign Affairs and Senate Foreign Relations at least 15 days before effectiveness that the waiver is CRUCIAL to U.S. national-security interests; this bill states no 180-day maximum. Existing IEEPA implementation and penalties apply to specified regulatory violations, preserving criminal willfulness/conviction rather than equating sanctions designation with a criminal judgment. Classified evidence may be submitted ex parte/in camera in an existing judicial review, without creating a review right. The recorded suspension-and-passage as amended concerns the entire EH, a separate bill episode; no actual piracy, designation, visa cancellation, removal, enforcement outcome or enactment is inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-06-23. This does not establish enactment.

- Presidential foreign-person/knowingly-piracy determination and State/DHS knowledge-or-reason-to-believe are distinct qualified predicates. Existing chapter81 offenses have their own elements and citizenship/jurisdiction limits; no automatic conviction requirement or universal offense applicability.
- Property blocking is discretionary; immigration restrictions are the conditional operative route. Citizen/permanent-resident/U.S.-entity exclusions are printed, but any-person-in-U.S. exclusion from other bills is not imported.
- Revocation is mandatory for the described alien and incorporates221(i), whose existing authority, notice, carrier-reliance and qualified review rules remain material. Admission, parole, visa documents and criminal/removal outcomes remain different.
- International obligations, qualified humanitarian transactions and authorized U.S. activities are excepted. The waiver requires CRUCIAL national security and15-day advance certification, with no printed180-day cap; no family extension is stated.
- Whole-bill amended suspension-and-passage does not prove a component preference, piracy guilt, actual sanction, document cancellation, removal or enactment.

### H.R.875 intoxicated/impaired-driving immigration grounds

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.875 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.875 would add jurisdiction-defined intoxicated/impaired-driving inadmissibility based on conviction or specified admissions of conduct, and conviction-based deportability within the admitted-person framework, regardless of misdemeanor/felony classification.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:183**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.875 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact H.R.875 EH proposal adds INA 212(a)(2)(J) inadmissibility for any alien convicted of, admitting commission of, or admitting acts constituting the essential elements of an intoxicated/impaired-driving offense. The terms take their meaning under the law of the jurisdiction where the conviction, offense or essential acts occurred, expressly including alcohol/drug influence or impairment, without regard to misdemeanor/felony classification under Federal, State, tribal or local law. Existing INA 212(a) makes inadmissibility relevant to visas and admission. It separately adds INA 237(a)(2)(G) deportability for conviction of a jurisdiction-defined intoxicated/impaired-driving offense, including alcohol/drug influence or impairment, regardless of the listed misdemeanor/felony classification. That clause uses the law where conviction occurred and does not make admission of conduct alone its predicate. Neither clause requires an injury/death or multiple offenses. The existing INA 101(a)(48) conviction framework remains, including specified withheld-adjudication situations with guilt/plea/facts and court-ordered punishment/penalty/restraint. These are additional categorical statutory grounds, rather than a claim that covered conduct previously could never trigger other criminal inadmissibility or deportability. Existing criminal INA grounds retain their own elements and exceptions. The single-offense minor/petty-offense exception in 212(a)(2)(A), the enumerated 212(h) waiver, and the 237(a)(2)(A)(vi) pardon exception are not printed exceptions to new J/G. Existing discretionary temporary-nonimmigrant/T/U inadmissibility waiver routes are retained within their own conditions; no categorical relief, guilt or automatic deportation is inferred. The bill concerns any alien meeting the grounds, not only a person unlawfully present. The new 237(a)(2) deportability ground sits within the existing 237(a) framework for an alien (including a crewman) in and admitted to the United States, with removal upon the specified government order. It is not a universal new deportability ground for every unadmitted alien. INA 101 distinguishes immigration admission (lawful entry after inspection/authorization) from admitting criminal conduct; parole alone is not admission. This governing status scope is separate from any offense's after-admission timing element. No individual admission status or actual removal order is inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-06-26. This does not establish enactment.

- INA 237(a) deportability concerns an alien in and admitted to the United States, subject to the governing order framework. Immigration admission and admissions of criminal conduct are different conditions; parole alone is not immigration admission. The new numbered ground does not discard this governing chapeau or necessarily add an after-admission-crime timing element.
- The new inadmissibility predicate is conviction or the specified admissions; the new deportability predicate requires conviction. Arrest alone is not either printed predicate, and admission of conduct alone does not establish new G deportability.
- Offense meanings follow the specified jurisdiction. Alcohol and drug impairment are expressly included; misdemeanor/felony classification is not a threshold. Neither new clause adds an injury/death, repeat-offense, imprisonment-length or after-admission condition of its own.
- Existing INA conviction rules and other criminal grounds remain. Their enumerated petty-offense, pardon or 212(h) exceptions are not automatically extended to these new grounds. Retained temporary-admission/T/U waiver authorities are conditioned and discretionary rather than universal relief.
- This is whole-bill House passage. It does not establish separate component preferences, actual guilt, visa/admission denial, removal or enactment; no preferred alternative follows from opposition.

### H.R.275 monthly specified-alien encounter reports

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.275 with those reporting requirements; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.275 would require monthly public/congressional DHS reports on specified potential-security-risk alien encounters, including nationality/residence, geography and port/between-port/interior distinctions, plus an initial historical-period report.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:184**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.275 with those reporting requirements; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact H.R.275 EH proposal requires the DHS Secretary, by the seventh day of each month starting with the first full month after enactment, to publish a DHS webpage report and submit it to the House Homeland Security and Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committees. Each report must give the preceding month's total encounters of “special interest aliens”, their nationalities or countries of last habitual residence, geographic disaggregation, whether encounters occurred at land/air/sea ports of entry, between ports or in the U.S. interior, and identification of covered-nation nationalities/residences. The first report also covers January 20, 2021 through January 19, 2025. The operative definition is an INA-defined alien who, based on analysis of travel patterns, potentially poses a national-security risk to the United States or its interests. It does not establish an actual threat, criminal guilt, inadmissibility or removal for an individual. The incorporated covered nations are North Korea, China, Russia and Iran under 10 USC 4872(f)(2); only that definition is imported, not the unrelated procurement powers. The operative report covers DHS encounters, including the interior. The title's reference to attempts at unlawful entry does not create an unstated unlawful-entry-only reporting limit. This bill adds this specific recurring and retrospective reporting duty; it neither changes admission/removal eligibility nor authorizes new detention or spending. No actual report, encounter totals, threat finding or enactment is inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-06-26. This does not establish enactment.

- The deadline is the seventh day of each month beginning with the first full post-enactment month, for the immediately preceding month. The first report additionally covers the fixed January 20, 2021–January 19, 2025 historical period.
- The reported category depends on travel-pattern analysis and potential security risk, not proof of guilt or actual threat. The INA alien definition excludes U.S. citizens/nationals. Covered-nation labeling reports four incorporated countries rather than creating admission sanctions.
- The exact operative scope includes ports, between-port encounters and interior encounters; unlawful attempted entry is not stated as an additional predicate in section 2(a). A measure title cannot narrow that scope.
- This is reporting/publication legislation, not actual enforcement, new detention or admission/removal eligibility. Whole-bill passage does not establish separate preferences about each data category, actual encounter numbers or enactment.

### H.R.747 fentanyl sanctions coverage and optional visa menu

**Compact:** Foushee supported passage of H.R.747 as a whole with its source-qualified provisions. H.R.747 would add qualified Chinese entities/officials to an existing sanctions scheme that includes an optional visa-exclusion menu; it extends a 2019-anchored reporting window, while leaving the separate seven-year termination provision unchanged.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:220**

Foushee supported passage of H.R.747 as a whole with its source-qualified provisions.

The exact H.R.747 EH directly changes the person criteria of the Fentanyl Sanctions Act rather than merely expressing drug-policy preferences. Existing2302(5) covers foreign persons presidentially determined to play a significant role in opioid trafficking. The proposal retains that route and adds PRC entities presidentially determined to produce/manufacture/distribute/sell or knowingly finance/transport the specified synthetic-opioid or production-ingredient/chemical goods AND fail credible steps, including know-your-customer or U.S. counternarcotics cooperation, to detect/prevent opioid trafficking. It also adds senior Chinese-government or other Chinese political officials with significant regulatory/law-enforcement responsibilities for such entities AND who aid/abet trafficking, including intentional inaction. This is not every Chinese person or producer and not a factual designation by the House. The existing definitions distinguish illicit opioid activities/attempts/assistance from lawful medicine: foreign person means foreign citizen/national or non-U.S.-organized entity, excluding FOREIGN GOVERNMENTS; it is not the blanket not-a-U.S.-person definition of H.R.36/1998. U.S.-person definitions separately include citizens/nationals, LPRs, U.S. entities and anyone within the United States for their identified statutory uses. Knowingly includes actual or should-have-known knowledge. The report must additionally determine WHETHER heads of the four named Chinese agencies are traffickers; those officials are not automatically listed as guilty. Existing2311 report, classified/public portions, already-sanctioned exclusions, protected intelligence/law-enforcement nondisclosures and consultation conditions remain. Section3(b) changes the REPORT-SUBMISSION period in2311(c) from five to ten years AFTER DECEMBER20,2019: the endpoint becomes December 20, 2029, not ten years after this proposal enactment. The bill does NOT amend2334, which separately terminates the chapter and sanctions seven years after December 20, 2019, December 20, 2026. This visible timing interaction is preserved for legal review; no inferred repeal or guarantee of sanctions through2029 is accepted. Existing2312 requires at least five menu sanctions for covered entities and four for covered individuals, including qualifying listed persons and the specified owned/controlled, precursor-supply/source and agency relationships. The menu has financial/procurement/exchange/bank/property/investment measures. Crucially2313(a)(8) MAY direct State to deny a visa and DHS to exclude an ALIEN presidentially determined to be a corporate officer/principal or controlling shareholder of the foreign person;2313(a)(9) MAY apply applicable menu measures to principal executives or similar-function individuals. Visa exclusion is an optional selected measure, not mandatory for every newly covered entity/official, and this menu does not itself add universal INA-benefit/parole bars, family sanctions or immediate cancellation of all entry documents. Existing intelligence/law-enforcement exceptions and necessary UN-headquarters/Vienna-consular/other international-obligation admission exception apply. Existing2314 preserves qualified state-owned-entity cooperation waivers at most 12 months, with15-day advance certification, domestic scheduling plus at least two listed cooperation improvements and similarly bounded renewals with State certification; national-security/access-to-prescription-medication waivers with required medication nontrafficking monitoring and15-day postdetermination notice; and humanitarian waivers renewable 180 days upon the required necessity certification. The candidate does not replace those with the waivers in another bill. Existing judicial-classified-evidence rules confer no new review right; existing agency-delegation notes are preserved, not a claim every presidential function is first assigned by H.R.747. Section4 adds at least annual written effectiveness/stakeholder/potential-change evaluation when IEEPA authority is exercised for an international-drug-trafficking emergency declared in the preceding five years and not terminated. It changes existing1703 consultation/report recipients from Congress to specified committees and adds cost/benefit, termination-criteria and explanatory duties for covered-emergency regulations under1704. Existing emergency powers, authority exceptions, immediate and six-month reporting and regulation authority predate the bill; the proposal is not first emergency authority or every emergency annualized. Section5 adds no importation-of-goods sanction authority. Its sense-of-Congress requests remain nonbinding. This amended suspension-and-passage choice includes the full sanctions/reporting/oversight package, with a separate bill episode; actual designation, visa denial, exclusion, agency guilt, trafficking conduct, removed status or enactment is not inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-09-02. This does not establish enactment.

- Eligibility is established through the explicit change to the existing sanctions person criteria and qualified optional visa menu, not through fentanyl rhetoric, the title or every financial sanction.
- New PRC entity criteria require the goods/activity and credible-step failure conjunction; official criteria require regulatory/law-enforcement responsibility AND aiding/abetting. Named agency-head reporting asks WHETHER, not automatic designation or proof of misconduct.
- Visa denial/exclusion is optional under the existing menu and has alien/corporate-officer/principal/controlling-shareholder or applicable principal-executive qualifications. No mandatory universal visa ban, parole/other-INA-benefit bar, family sanction or document-revocation rule is invented.
- The ten-year reporting endpoint uses the original December 20, 2019 anchor. Unchanged2334 separately terminates the chapter/sanctions December 20, 2026. Legal timing interaction remains a review dependency; no silent extension of all sanction authority through2029.
- Existing foreign-person/U.S.-person definitions, waiver classes, periods, monitoring, notices and exceptions remain distinct. Covered emergency evaluation/regulation duties are qualified; existing consultation/immediate/six-month reporting and IEEPA powers remain.
- Whole-bill amended suspension-and-passage does not prove a component preference, actual trafficking, list placement, visa denial, exclusion, deportation, enforcement effect or enactment.

### S.5: expanding specified immigration detention and State enforcement litigation

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of S.5 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. S.5 would expand mandatory immigration custody and DHS detainers for the specified inadmissibility/offense combination (burglary, theft, larceny, shoplifting, assault of a law-enforcement officer, or any crime resulting in another person's death or serious bodily injury), and add harm-conditioned State suits over specified detention, release, parole and visa duties.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:23**

Foushee opposed passage of S.5 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

S.5 would extend the federal mandatory-custody category to the following offenses: burglary, theft, larceny, shoplifting, assault of a law-enforcement officer, or any crime resulting in another person's death or serious bodily injury. The listed property-offense terms, assault of a law-enforcement officer and serious bodily injury take the meanings assigned by the jurisdiction where the acts occurred. The exact bill adds an INA 236(c) mandatory-custody category requiring inadmissibility under INA 212(a)(6)(A), (6)(C), or (7) together with a specified criminal predicate: being charged, arrested, convicted, admitting commission, or admitting acts constituting the essential elements. These inadmissibility categories concern presence without admission/parole or undesignated arrival, material immigration fraud/false citizenship claims, and specified entry-document defects, subject to their existing statutory qualifications. DHS must issue a detainer for a person meeting the new ground and, if the person is not otherwise detained by Federal, State or local officials, effectively and expeditiously take custody. The bill also gives a State attorney general or other authorized State officer standing, on alleged harm to the State or residents (including financial harm in excess of $100), to seek appropriate injunctive relief in district court over five specified federal matters: INA 235(b)(1)/(2) detention/removal requirements; INA 236 release, bond or parole decisions; the existing INA 243(d) visa-discontinuance requirement for countries denying or unreasonably delaying acceptance; violation of the existing INA 212(d)(5)(A) case-by-case urgent-humanitarian/significant-public-benefit parole limits; and INA 241(a)(2) detention during the removal period. Courts must advance and expedite these cases to the greatest practicable extent. INA 236(e) removes its express release/grant shield while leaving the other printed discretionary-judgment/detention/revocation/denial restrictions, and INA 242(f)(1) receives an exception for the printed listed actions, while its separate final-removal-order injunction standard remains. These review changes do not establish a general right to enjoin every immigration action. The parole clause changes its named administering actor to the Secretary of Homeland Security. The baseline removal period is generally 90 days with specified starting/extension conditions; the bill adds litigation standing over its detention requirement rather than creating that period. The pre-existing narrow protection-release exception remains, redesignated as INA 236(c)(4), including its witness/potential-witness/cooperator and immediate-family/close-associate categories, no-danger/appearance conditions and offense-severity procedure.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-01-22. This does not establish enactment.

- The listed property-offense terms, assault of a law-enforcement officer and serious bodily injury take the meanings assigned by the jurisdiction where the acts occurred.
- The new custody ground requires both inadmissibility under INA 212(a)(6)(A), (6)(C), or (7) and a listed offense predicate. Arrest or charge can suffice for the offense predicate; conviction is not required. Existing inadmissibility exceptions and waiver routes are not repealed, including specified battered-person/false-citizenship exceptions and document/fraud waivers.
- The retained INA 236(c) release exception, redesignated as (c)(4), can protect a witness, potential witness, person cooperating with an investigation into major criminal activity, or an immediate family member or close associate of one. The Attorney General must decide release is necessary under 18 USC 3521 protection, and the person must show no danger and likely appearance; the decision procedure considers offense severity. Ordinary bond/parole is not substituted for these conditions. The incorporated protection program retains its serious-case/threat, public-risk assessment and memorandum requirements, with the printed urgent-temporary-protection procedure; it is not automatic protection or release.
- State litigation requires alleged harm to the State or residents, including financial harm in excess of $100. Relief is appropriate injunctive relief and expedited handling to the greatest practicable extent; a filed suit or favorable judgment is not inferred.
- Case-by-case humanitarian/significant-public-benefit parole limits and the conditional visa-discontinuance duty for countries refusing or delaying acceptance already exist. The bill adds State enforcement standing and related review changes; it does not newly create those substantive baseline limits.
- The visa-discontinuance duty arises after the statutory federal notification that a foreign country denies or unreasonably delays acceptance after a request. It covers immigrant visas, nonimmigrant visas, or both for the specified nationals/residents in that country, and lasts until the statutory acceptance notice. These conditions are retained, not newly imposed.
- The existing INA 236(c)(1) when-released framework remains; the new DHS detainer clause separately requires expeditious custody when the person is not otherwise detained. No actual detention timing or individual outcome is inferred.
- This is a whole-bill House passage choice. It does not establish separate component positions, guilt, actual detention/removal, a successful suit or enactment. The parallel House and Senate bills remain distinct proposal episodes; later enacted text does not replace either exact voting version.

### H.R.3486 improper-entry and removed-person reentry penalties

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.3486 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.3486 would raise specified improper-entry/reentry penalties and add minimum prison terms for qualified post-entry or pre-removal convictions, while preserving consent exceptions and the collateral-review conditions.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:264**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.3486 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact H.R.3486 EH proposal raises the INA 275(a) subsequent improper-entry offense imprisonment maximum from 2 to 5 years; the existing first-offense 6-month maximum/fine-or-both and other 275 provisions remain. New 275(e) covers an alien who enters/attempts entry at an undesignated time/place, eludes immigration examination/inspection, or attempts/obtains entry through willfully false/misleading representation or concealment of a material fact, and thereafter is convicted of a crime punishable by more than one year. That conjunction carries mandatory imprisonment of at least 5 years up to life, with an optional title 18 fine. Mere unlawful presence, arrest or all first entries do not alone satisfy it. INA 276(a) continues to require prior denial of admission/exclusion/deportation/removal or departure while a removal-type order is outstanding, followed by entry/attempt or being found in the United States. It raises the general maximum from 2 to 10 years, with fine-or-both alternatives. It preserves advance express consent to reapply and the specified prior-denial/removal no-advance-consent exception, naming DHS rather than the Attorney General. Rewritten 276(b), except where new(c) applies, raises the maximum for pre-removal conviction of at least 3 drug/person misdemeanors from 10 to 15 years. It retains the specified INA 235(c)/TitleV exclusion/removal unauthorized reentry category's 10-year nonconcurrent prison term plus fine, and the qualified 241(a)(4)(B) early-removal reentry category's 10-year maximum/fine-or-both. It adds a category for at least 3 prior denials/exclusions/deportations/removals followed by entry/attempt/being found, with 10-year maximum/fine-or-both. New 276(c) overrides(a)/(b) penalties for a covered reentrant convicted before removal/departure of an aggravated felony, any crime classed as a felony by the relevant Federal/State/Tribal/local jurisdiction, or any crime punishable by more than one year: mandatory imprisonment of at least 10 years up to life, with an optional fine. This replaces the prior felony/aggravated-felony maximums rather than changing every felony into an INA aggravated felony. The existing INA aggravated-felony definition and its offense-specific/foreign-offense qualifications remain. Stipulated-removal agreements during or outside Federal/State criminal trials count as removal for(b)/(c). The existing remainder-of-unserved-sentence reentry provision is redesignated(d), with its removal cross-reference and administering actor updated. Existing collateral attack on the underlying order remains redesignated(e), requiring exhaustion of available administrative remedies, improper deprivation of judicial review and fundamental unfairness. These are legislative penalty/actor/cross-reference changes; individual guilt, validity of a removal order, sentence, actual enforcement or enactment is not inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-09-11. This does not establish enactment.

- INA 275(a) changes the subsequent-offense maximum, not the first-offense maximum. New 275(e) separately requires a specified improper-entry predicate followed by conviction of a crime punishable by more than one year; it is not a minimum for mere unlawful presence or every first entry.
- General 276 reentry retains its prior-removal-type event and subsequent entry/attempt/found-in-U.S. conditions plus specified advance-consent exceptions. DHS is the named consent actor in the rewritten text.
- New 276(c) is an overriding minimum 10-years-to-life category for the three alternative pre-removal conviction predicates. Other printed categories retain fine-or-both alternatives; the specified terrorism-removal reentry category requires the 10-year term to run nonconcurrently.
- Aggravated felony uses the retained INA definition, including its separate offense thresholds and foreign-offense 15-year completion qualification; the bill also independently covers jurisdiction-defined felonies and crimes punishable by more than one year. It does not collapse those alternatives into one definition.
- The prior unfinished prison term rule and three conjunctive collateral-order-challenge conditions remain, redesignated rather than repealed. A House vote does not adjudicate an individual order, consent, conviction or sentence.
- This is complete bill passage, not separate votes on entry, reentry or any penalty tier; actual incarceration, removal, enforcement or enactment is not established.

### H.R.4371 unaccompanied-child placement, sponsor and household-information rules

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.4371 as a whole with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.4371 would change unaccompanied-child placement and sponsor checks, require secure placement for specified children aged 12 or older, and require sponsor/adult-household data sharing. Its replacement omits existing monthly-review and age-18 transfer clauses; retained safeguards and printed reference issues remain visible.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:340**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.4371 as a whole with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

The complete H.R.4371 EH changes placement, sponsor suitability and information-sharing for unaccompanied alien children in the existing HHS/ORR immigration-custody framework. Existing 6 USC 279(g)(2) defines that child class by no lawful U.S. immigration status, age under 18, and no U.S. parent/legal guardian or none available to provide care/physical custody; it is not every noncitizen child or every child at a border. The bill changes ORR placement determinations to follow the replaced1232(c)(2). HHS may not make the initial placement determination for a child in Federal custody because of immigration status until it consults DHS/AG, including appropriate juvenile-justice officials, about proceedings attendance, protection from exploitation/gangs and flight/danger/criminal-record questions. For children 12 or older HHS must contact the relevant citizenship/nationality/last-residence consulate or embassy to obtain relevant arrest/charge/conviction records and examine gang-related tattoos/markings. The bill requires those processes, not a factual finding that every child has a record or a guarantee foreign authorities supply one. It generally retains prompt placement in the least restrictive best-interest setting EXCEPT as otherwise provided, and the no-own-recognizance prohibition (which already exists in279(b)(2), not first created here). For a child12or older, it requires secure-facility placement for the duration of immigration proceedings and, if removal is ordered, until removal, when the child is a flight risk or a danger to self/others/community. The danger branch includes a gang tattoo/marking; conviction of a serious criminal offense in the specified U.S. or citizenship/nationality/last-residence jurisdictions; conviction of an INA aggravated felony; or gang-affiliation/activity conduct in those jurisdictions with any arrest record, pending charge, other pending proceeding or conviction. These statutory triggers do not establish factual gang membership, guilt or actual danger from a House vote. The twelve-year threshold limits these screening/secure-placement branches, not all sponsor or household requirements. The imported serious-offense definition is INA101(h): felony, referenced crime of violence, or the specified reckless/impaired-driving crime involving injury to another. INA aggravated felony remains its defined category with its own offense, sentence and foreign-completion-period conditions, not every local felony. Section16(a) elements and printed16(b) residual text are not treated as interchangeable or every printed residual clause as currently enforceable: the official Dimaya opinion holds16(b) impermissibly vague in the INA aggravated-felony context. The candidate preserves that constraint; application of incorporated definitions to this novel placement rule, including its broader felony alternative, is a review dependency, not an individual classification. The full replacement of1232(c)(2) OMITS the old monthly secure-placement-review requirement, the express child-trafficking-victim Unaccompanied Refugee Minor placement option when suitable family is unavailable, and the old age 18 HHS-to-DHS transfer least-restrictive-setting/alternative-detention eligibility language. The general best-interest rule is restated with new exceptions, but those omitted clauses are not silently described as retained. This does not repeal every regulatory/judicial review right, every adult custody authority or all refugee-minor assistance;279(b)(3) foster-system encouragement and separately qualified1232(d)(4) assistance remain. Sponsor placement is barred for an individual who is not a U.S. citizen or lawful permanent resident and for an individual who has, or shares a household with someone who has, a conviction in any of eleven printed crime categories. Those categories include defined sex offenses; severe trafficking; domestic violence; child abuse/neglect; murder/manslaughter/attempt; specified minor-sexual-depiction crimes; the incorporated236(c)(1) crime category; aggravated felonies; any jurisdiction-defined felony; any crime punishable by more than one year; or another AG-designated crime in sole/unreviewable discretion. The CONVICTION gate applies to sponsor/household categories, unlike the child gang-record branch. SORNA sex-offense foreign-fairness/consensual-conduct exclusions and minor-offense definitions are preserved; the reference to111(5) does not silently import111(8) juvenile-conviction treatment into every crime category. Domestic violence must be criminal, not every noncriminal victim-services behavior. CAPTA child-abuse/neglect definitions include the qualified child/parent-caretaker/serious-harm or imminent-risk scope, but the bill still requires a crime conviction, not a mere civil allegation. Referenced homicide/attempt/2252 definitions retain their own elements and jurisdiction/knowing/actual-minor/defense qualifications; no automatic finding that every similarly named offense satisfies them. The sponsor236(c)(1) crime reference is read with enacted119-1 custody-list additions and its underlying INA grounds; the bill own conviction requirement is not replaced by those custody provisions arrest/charge/admission alternatives, nor is a sponsor automatically required to be a detainable alien when the bill expressly applies to any individual. Before placement with an individual, HHS must give DHS sponsor AND EACH ADULT household-resident name, SSN or ITIN, birth date, residence location/address, immigration status, contact details including available work number, and all background/criminal-check results. At minimum checks include the Dru Sjodin sex-offender website, public-records check and fingerprint-based national criminal-history check. Existing 1232(c)(3) capable-custodian determination, identity/relationship verification, independent risk finding, mandatory qualified home studies/follow-up and DHS-to-HHS suitability information remain, now subject to the new3(D) data requirement; counsel/orientation/child-advocate provisions are not rewritten. ORR general placement/care duties and the bar on transferring other agencies INA-benefit adjudication remain.279(b)(4) no-bond construction is not expressly amended, but its2(B) reference points into the replaced paragraph and is preserved as a legal-reference dependency, not silently repaired or treated as a universal new bond duty. The bill applies on enactment to pending/future release/custody determinations and all release redeterminations, but prints HomelandSecurityAct642(g)(2) in that applicability definition while the actual existing child definition is462(g)(2). This mismatch remains visible for review, not accepted as a repaired label. Maximum-lawful-effect/severability language does not determine a court outcome. Its PRA/APA provisions are conditional on named officials determining compliance would impede immediate implementation; they do not declare every agency procedure or review permanently exempt. This separate whole-bill final-passage episode establishes only the recorded choice on the complete proposal, not a sponsor-specific view, motive, actual child risk/conviction, detention/placement outcome, removal order or enactment.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-12-16. This does not establish enactment.

- The source-defined unaccompanied-child class is narrower than all noncitizen children. Twelve-and-older consular/tattoo screening and secure-placement conditions are distinct from all-age sponsor/household requirements; contact duties do not guarantee foreign records or prove gang membership.
- Flight/danger and printed child tattoo/conviction/gang-record predicates are distinct; arrest or a pending proceeding is not guilt. Referenced serious/aggravated/crime categories retain their legal elements and judicial constraints. Dimaya16(b) limits are preserved; novel placement applications are not adjudicated.
- Whole1232(c)(2) replacement omits the old monthly review, express trafficking-victim refugee-minor option and age18transfer/alternative-detention language. Restated least-restrictive best-interest placement has new exceptions; other qualified assistance, home studies/counsel/advocate and procedural safeguards are not all repealed.
- Sponsor/household crime categories require conviction, unlike some child gang-record triggers. Citizen/LPR qualification and EACH ADULT household data/check sharing are independent rules; no assertion every arrest, noncriminal abuse allegation or juvenile adjudication satisfies all sponsor bars.
- Existing no-own-recognizance/screening/protection duties predate the bill. No express amendment to279(b)(4) is inferred from its dangling2(B) reference; no silent repair of the printed642(g)(2) applicability label or individual child/bond ruling.
- Named-official PRA/APA implementation language is conditional, and construction/severability is not proof of constitutional validity. Whole-proposal choice does not establish preferred alternatives, actual detention/removal/placement, crime, family risk or enactment.

### H.R.35 immigration consequences for specified border vehicle flight

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.35 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.35 would create a specified intentional vehicle-flight offense within 100 miles of the U.S. border and add conviction/conduct-admission-based inadmissibility, deportability within the admitted-person framework, and a separate immigration-relief bar including asylum.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:42**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.35 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact House-passed H.R.35 adds 18 USC 40B: operating a motor vehicle within 100 miles of the United States border while intentionally fleeing a pursuing U.S. Border Patrol agent acting under lawful authority, or a pursuing Federal/State/local officer actively assisting or under Border Patrol command. The ordinary penalty alternatives are imprisonment up to 2 years, a title 18 fine, or both; where serious bodily injury results the imprisonment alternative is 5–20 years, and where any person's death results it is 10 years to life, with fine-or-both alternatives retained in both cases. The bill adds INA 212(a)(2)(J) inadmissibility and 237(a)(2)(G) deportability for any alien convicted of, admitting commission of, or admitting acts constituting the essential elements of 40B(a). It also inserts 208A making a person meeting that same predicate ineligible for relief under the immigration laws, expressly including asylum under 208. Neither arrest alone nor merely being within 100 miles is the printed immigration predicate. Pre-existing 18 USC 758 already criminalizes vehicle flight/evasion from an immigration or other federal checkpoint followed by fleeing law-enforcement agents above the legal speed limit, with up-to 5 years/fine/both; existing 237(a)(2)(A)(iv) already makes a conviction for 758 deportable. H.R.35 creates a different officer/geography/intent predicate and additional immigration consequences, rather than the first-ever immigration checkpoint-flight offense or deportability ground. Unlike that existing deportability ground, the newG also accepts specified admissions. Existing INA 101(a)(48) conviction rules remain. The Attorney General, with the Secretary of Homeland Security, must report annually to both Judiciary Committees on violations, charged/apprehended/not-charged/not-apprehended counts, and penalties sought/imposed. A reporting duty is not evidence of actual violations, convictions or enforcement. The separate broad relief-bar clause is preserved as printed; it is not replaced by a blanket claim that existing narrow waivers guarantee relief, and no individual asylum or removal outcome is decided. The new 237(a)(2) deportability ground sits within the existing 237(a) framework for an alien (including a crewman) in and admitted to the United States, with removal upon the specified government order. It is not a universal new deportability ground for every unadmitted alien. INA 101 distinguishes immigration admission (lawful entry after inspection/authorization) from admitting criminal conduct; parole alone is not admission. This governing status scope is separate from any offense's after-admission timing element. No individual admission status or actual removal order is inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-02-13. This does not establish enactment.

- INA 237(a) deportability concerns an alien in and admitted to the United States, subject to the governing order framework. Immigration admission and admissions of criminal conduct are different conditions; parole alone is not immigration admission. The new numbered ground does not discard this governing chapeau or necessarily add an after-admission-crime timing element. The separate 208A relief-bar clause uses its own any-alien predicate; the 237(a) status boundary is not silently substituted for that broader clause.
- The criminal predicate requires vehicle operation, within 100 miles of the U.S. border, intentional flight and the specified pursuing officer relationship/lawful Border Patrol authority. The bill does not state a legal-speed-limit or checkpoint element for new 40B; those are elements of the separate existing 758 offense.
- The imprisonment ranges retain fine-or-both alternatives in the printed clauses. Serious bodily injury or death must result from commission of the offense for the corresponding enhanced alternative; actual harm or guilt is not established here.
- New inadmissibility, deportability and relief ineligibility require conviction or the specified admissions of commission/essential acts. They do not follow merely from an arrest. The 208A relief bar expressly includes asylum; the candidate preserves its breadth without deciding individual relief/waiver interactions or actual deportation.
- Existing high-speed checkpoint-flight law and its conviction-based deportability are retained, including the old 237(a)(2)(A)(vi) pardon exception for that ground. That enumerated exception is not automatically applied to newG. H.R.35 adds distinct grounds; newJ/G labels are evaluated within this exact proposal, rather than combined with same-numbered grounds in parallel pending bills.
- This is a whole-bill House passage choice. It does not establish separate component preferences, enactment, actual enforcement, admission denial, asylum refusal or removal. The annual report provision requires reporting rather than creating actual enforcement outcomes.

### H.R.29: expanding specified immigration detention and State enforcement litigation

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.29 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.29 would expand mandatory immigration custody and DHS detainers for the specified inadmissibility/offense combination (burglary, theft, larceny or shoplifting), and add harm-conditioned State suits over specified detention, release, parole and visa duties.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:6**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.29 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

H.R.29 would extend the federal mandatory-custody category to the following offenses: burglary, theft, larceny or shoplifting. The four listed property-offense terms take the meaning assigned by the jurisdiction where the acts occurred. The exact bill adds an INA 236(c) mandatory-custody category requiring inadmissibility under INA 212(a)(6)(A), (6)(C), or (7) together with a specified criminal predicate: being charged, arrested, convicted, admitting commission, or admitting acts constituting the essential elements. These inadmissibility categories concern presence without admission/parole or undesignated arrival, material immigration fraud/false citizenship claims, and specified entry-document defects, subject to their existing statutory qualifications. DHS must issue a detainer for a person meeting the new ground and, if the person is not otherwise detained by Federal, State or local officials, effectively and expeditiously take custody. The bill also gives a State attorney general or other authorized State officer standing, on alleged harm to the State or residents (including financial harm in excess of $100), to seek appropriate injunctive relief in district court over five specified federal matters: INA 235(b)(1)/(2) detention/removal requirements; INA 236 release, bond or parole decisions; the existing INA 243(d) visa-discontinuance requirement for countries denying or unreasonably delaying acceptance; violation of the existing INA 212(d)(5)(A) case-by-case urgent-humanitarian/significant-public-benefit parole limits; and INA 241(a)(2) detention during the removal period. Courts must advance and expedite these cases to the greatest practicable extent. INA 236(e) removes its express release/grant shield while leaving the other printed discretionary-judgment/detention/revocation/denial restrictions, and INA 242(f)(1) receives an exception for the printed listed actions, while its separate final-removal-order injunction standard remains. These review changes do not establish a general right to enjoin every immigration action. The parole clause changes its named administering actor to the Secretary of Homeland Security. The baseline removal period is generally 90 days with specified starting/extension conditions; the bill adds litigation standing over its detention requirement rather than creating that period. The pre-existing narrow protection-release exception remains, redesignated as INA 236(c)(4), including its witness/potential-witness/cooperator and immediate-family/close-associate categories, no-danger/appearance conditions and offense-severity procedure.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-01-07. This does not establish enactment.

- The four listed property-offense terms take the meaning assigned by the jurisdiction where the acts occurred.
- The new custody ground requires both inadmissibility under INA 212(a)(6)(A), (6)(C), or (7) and a listed offense predicate. Arrest or charge can suffice for the offense predicate; conviction is not required. Existing inadmissibility exceptions and waiver routes are not repealed, including specified battered-person/false-citizenship exceptions and document/fraud waivers.
- The retained INA 236(c) release exception, redesignated as (c)(4), can protect a witness, potential witness, person cooperating with an investigation into major criminal activity, or an immediate family member or close associate of one. The Attorney General must decide release is necessary under 18 USC 3521 protection, and the person must show no danger and likely appearance; the decision procedure considers offense severity. Ordinary bond/parole is not substituted for these conditions. The incorporated protection program retains its serious-case/threat, public-risk assessment and memorandum requirements, with the printed urgent-temporary-protection procedure; it is not automatic protection or release.
- State litigation requires alleged harm to the State or residents, including financial harm in excess of $100. Relief is appropriate injunctive relief and expedited handling to the greatest practicable extent; a filed suit or favorable judgment is not inferred.
- Case-by-case humanitarian/significant-public-benefit parole limits and the conditional visa-discontinuance duty for countries refusing or delaying acceptance already exist. The bill adds State enforcement standing and related review changes; it does not newly create those substantive baseline limits.
- The visa-discontinuance duty arises after the statutory federal notification that a foreign country denies or unreasonably delays acceptance after a request. It covers immigrant visas, nonimmigrant visas, or both for the specified nationals/residents in that country, and lasts until the statutory acceptance notice. These conditions are retained, not newly imposed.
- The existing INA 236(c)(1) when-released framework remains; the new DHS detainer clause separately requires expeditious custody when the person is not otherwise detained. No actual detention timing or individual outcome is inferred.
- This is a whole-bill House passage choice. It does not establish separate component positions, guilt, actual detention/removal, a successful suit or enactment. The parallel House and Senate bills remain distinct proposal episodes; later enacted text does not replace either exact voting version.

### H.R.495 annual cross-border-tunnel strategic-plan reports

**Compact:** Foushee supported suspending the rules and passing H.R.495 to add annual implementation reports on the existing cross-border-tunnel strategic plan. H.R.495 would add annual CBP reports on implementation of the existing illicit cross-border-tunnel strategic plan after its initial report deadline; it does not newly establish that plan or authorize additional funding.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:63**

Foushee supported suspending the rules and passing H.R.495 to add annual implementation reports on the existing cross-border-tunnel strategic plan.

The exact H.R.495 EH proposal adds “and annually thereafter” to Public Law 117-263 section 7134(a)(2), after “development of the strategic plan”. The Commissioner of U.S. Customs and Border Protection would therefore report on implementation of the existing counter illicit cross-border tunnel operations strategic plan not later than one year after its development and annually thereafter, to the House Homeland Security and Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committees. The pre-existing 7134(a)(1) already requires the Commissioner, coordinating with the DHS Science and Technology Under Secretary and appropriate DHS officials, to develop the plan within 180 days of the 2022law. Its existing scope includes risk-based identification/breach/assessment/remediation criteria, technologies and community impacts, information sharing, Border Patrol sector indicators and technology/staffing needs. H.R.495 changes the reporting cadence, not those plan requirements. The separate 6 USC 257 annual report on discovered Mexico–U.S. tunnels and DHS prevention/investigation/prosecution needs already exists and is not the provision amended here. Existing 7134(b) authorized$1 million for each of fiscal 2023 and 2024 for plan development and remediation; H.R.495 neither extends that authorization nor supplies new appropriations. Membership follows direct border-tunnel operations reporting. Actual reporting, tunnel remediation, border outcomes or enactment are not established by the House suspension-and-passage vote.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-03-10. This does not establish enactment.

- Only 7134(a)(2) report frequency changes. The strategic plan, initial deadline, responsible Commissioner, recipients and substantive plan scope are pre-existing; a separate annual tunnel-discovery/needs report in 6 USC 257 is retained.
- This is a report-on-implementation requirement, not a new order to deploy technologies, remediate tunnels or create the strategic plan. The existing fiscal 2023/2024 authorization is not renewed or converted into actual spending by this bill.
- The exact question is suspension of the rules and passage, not a vote on a separate border-operation component. Yea/Nay concern that complete proposition; actual implementation, efficacy, spending or enactment is not inferred.

### H.R.993 border-technology plan and innovation-team authority

**Compact:** Foushee supported suspending the rules and passing H.R.993 with its border-technology planning, innovation-team authority and procedures/reporting. H.R.993 would require a 180-day border-technology plan, authorize CBP innovation teams to research/adapt emerging commercial technologies, and require team procedures, metrics and reports; it does not require universal deployment or appropriate funds.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:65**

Foushee supported suspending the rules and passing H.R.993 with its border-technology planning, innovation-team authority and procedures/reporting.

The exact H.R.993 EH proposal requires the DHS Secretary, acting through the CBP Commissioner and DHS Science and Technology Under Secretary, to submit a plan within 180 days of enactment to the House Homeland Security and Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committees. The plan is to identify, integrate and deploy emerging/advanced technologies to enhance or address gaps in border-security operations. Its prescribed contents cover innovation-team use and composition, contributions and coordination, technologies from other agencies, procurement authorities and need for alternatives, scaling programs, program goals/timelines, border-community privacy/security impacts, replacement costs, research/private-sector coordination, metrics and listed aircraft/surveillance/inspection/tunnel/communications technologies. The bill gives the CBP Commissioner express authority to maintain one or more innovation teams to research and adapt new/emerging commercial technologies for border capability gaps and urgent mission needs, and assess potential outcomes including negative consequences where documented gaps are yet to be determined. It requires each maintained team to establish operating procedures, defined roles/partner relationships and transition protocols, plus strategic planning with costs, timeframes, metrics and performance parameters, including technology-transition rates. The Commissioner must report within 180 days and annually thereafter to the same committees on team procedures/plans, technologies piloted in the preceding fiscal year, success/gap determinations and status of transitioning successful technologies. This provides statutory authority and prescribed planning/reporting for maintaining teams; it does not establish that innovation teams or pilots first come into existence with this proposal. The technology list and planned deployment goals are not universal mandates to deploy every technology. No appropriation, universal pilot success or actual operational outcome is stated. Membership follows the express direct border-operations scope rather than DHS administration alone.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-03-10. This does not establish enactment.

- The 180-day plan submission is assigned to the DHS Secretary through the CBP Commissioner and Science and Technology Under Secretary. The separate 180-day/annual team report is assigned to the Commissioner; both go to the two specified Homeland Security committees.
- Team maintenance is authorized rather than mandatory creation of a particular number. Operating procedures/plans are required for maintained teams; their authority includes research/adaptation and assessing possible negative outcomes even before documented gaps are determined.
- Plan contents include privacy/security impacts on border communities, procurement-authority analysis, cost estimates, timeframes and transition metrics. The list of technologies is not an order to deploy every technology, and reports on pilots are not proof of successful or universal deployment.
- The bill supplies statutory team authority and planning/reporting requirements, not a claim that teams are historically new. It does not provide appropriations or establish actual spending, procurement, deployment, effectiveness or enactment.
- This exact suspension-and-passage vote concerns the complete bill. It does not establish separate preferences about artificial intelligence, surveillance, individual technologies or any plan component.

### H.R.23 conditional ICC-related immigration sanctions

**Compact:** Foushee opposed passage of H.R.23 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established. H.R.23 would require conditional ICC-related sanctions, including visa/admission/parole and other INA-benefit restrictions and immediate entry-document revocation for designated foreign actors and specified immediate relatives, subject to waiver and termination provisions.

**Detail:**

**house:119:1:7**

Foushee opposed passage of H.R.23 with those provisions; no preferred alternative is established.

The exact House-passed H.R.23 requires the President, not later than 60 days after enactment and on an ongoing basis, to impose sanctions if the ICC is attempting to investigate, arrest, detain or prosecute a protected person. Covered foreign persons are those the President determines directly engage in or aid such an effort; materially assist, sponsor or provide specified support/goods/services for it; or are owned/controlled by, or currently act or purport to have acted for, a person directly engaging in it. For covered aliens, the bill makes them inadmissible, ineligible for visas or other entry documents, admission or parole, and any other INA benefit. Their visas/entry documents must be revoked regardless of issuance date, effective immediately and automatically canceling other valid visas/entry documents in their possession. The immigration sanctions also apply to each sanctioned foreign person's spouse, parent, sibling or adult child; the separate property-blocking requirement applies to the principal foreign persons described in 3(a)(1). Foreign person means someone other than the defined United States person, which includes U.S. citizens, lawful permanent residents, U.S.-organized entities and any person in the United States. The incorporated INA alien definition excludes U.S. citizens and nationals. Protected persons include any defined U.S. person unless the United States both formally consents to ICC jurisdiction and is a Rome Statute party. They also include foreign citizens or lawful residents of a NATO or designated major non-NATO ally that has not consented to ICC jurisdiction or is not a Rome Statute party. The listed military/government personnel are inclusive examples, not the entire protected class. The President may waive sanctions case by case for periods no longer than 90 days each on a reported vital-national-security determination before the waiver takes effect, including the specified detailed rationale and actions by the United States and ICC. The President may terminate sanctions on written certification that the ICC has ceased all covered efforts and permanently closed/withdrawn/ended/terminated them. Imposition also requires congressional briefing/written notice within 10 days. The package includes property blocking under existing IEEPA authorities and penalties, rescission of available ICC appropriations at enactment and a prospective ban on using appropriated funds for the ICC. Immigration membership follows the direct travel/admission/parole/benefit restrictions, not the foreign-affairs findings. These are proposed legislative requirements; existing general INA inadmissibility/temporary-admission authorities are not treated as newly created by this bill. Actual designation, visa revocation, sanctions, enforcement or enactment is not inferred.

The Clerk recorded the House result as 'Passed' on 2025-01-09. This does not establish enactment.

- The trigger is an ICC attempt against a defined protected person plus the President's specified actor determination. It is not an unconditional travel ban on every ICC employee or foreign national. Protected-person conditions distinguish U.S. persons from citizens/lawful residents of defined allies.
- Immediate relatives mean spouse, parent, sibling or adult child. Immigration sanctions extend to relatives who meet the INA alien definition; the principal-target foreign-person limitation is not assumed to exclude every lawful-permanent-resident relative. Property blocking in 3(b)(1) refers to the principal 3(a)(1) actors.
- Visa/entry-document revocation is immediate and cancels other valid entry documents regardless of issuance date. No individual designation or actual entry refusal is established by a House vote.
- Waivers are case by case, at most 90 days each, with a vital-national-security determination and a specified pre-effect congressional report. Termination requires written certification of both cessation and permanent ending of all protected-person efforts; ordinary general INA discretion is not substituted for this specific sanctions waiver.
- This is whole-bill passage with property/funding provisions as well as immigration sanctions. It does not establish separate approval of each component, endorse the bill's findings, or establish actual spending, sanctioning, revocation or enactment.
